30	nontapuṇ ataṉilē vantiṭum nūṟiṭi.
	tanti tēṟiṭat taṉtuyar maṟittu
	makiḻcci kāṭṭiṉāṉ. vantatit taḷarcci.
	mūṭiṭil tīyum mūḷumum maṭaṅkāy.
2-m tōḻi:	ettaṉi vētaṉi! ettaṉi cōtaṉi!
35	yātu maṟiyāṭ kētit tuṇipu?
	ōtiya kaṭṭuri orumuṟi iṉiyum
	naviluti akkāḷ!
mutaṟṟōḻi:			naṅkinaṉ moḻiyeṉ 
	ceviyiṭi iṉiyum maṇipōl tikaḻum! 
	aracaṉi aṭipaṇin torucār otuṅki 
40	nīkkamil aṉpum ūkkamum kaḷippum
	kāṭṭiya matimukam kōṭṭiyē niṉṟa 
	tōṟṟameṉ kaṇṇiṉ māṟṟutal aritē!
	“eṉṉō itaṟkum yōcaṉi entāy!
	koṉṉē varuntali! koḷkiyiṟ piṟaḻā 
45	nītinam pāleṉil nērvatu jayamē.
	ētataṟ kiyam? ituviṭ ṭaṭimi
	peyarvatu peritala, pērula kataṟkut
	tuyarvarum ellinam tuyarnōk kutalō
	perumi! aṇṇitē muṉiyiṭam; karutiya 
50	pirivō orutiṉam! kuruvum tantiyum
	camameṉiṟ cuntara vimalaṉ taṉtirup
	pātā taravē pōtā tōtuṇi?
	āyiṉum attaṉi avacikam āyiṉ,
	ākuka āññip paṭiyē! taṭiyili.
55	aṉṉiyum niṉṉi aṉṟivē ṟaṟiyēṉ.
	uṉṉatē ivvuṭal. uṉtiru uḷḷam
	uṉṉiya paṭiyelām uvappac ceykuvaṉ.
	aṭimiyiṉ kavaliyāl aracark kiyalpām
	kaṭamiyiṟ piṟaḻum kalakkam vilakkuvi!
60	aṉpām uṉpāl aiya! uṉmakaḷ
	vēṇṭum varamelām yāṇṭumiv voṉṟē.”
2-m tōḻi:	moḻiyō ituvum? āā! āā!
	ituvelām kāṇavō eḻutiṉaṉ piramaṉ?
mutaṟṟōḻi:	etuvelām kāṇavō iruppatu ikkaṇ?
2-m tōḻi:	eṉcey kiṉṟaṉaḷ ippōtu ēḻi?
mutaṟṟōḻi:	vañciyiv aṟiyē varuvaḷ valli!
	ētō eḻutu kiṉṟaṉaḷ. vāṇi
	kōtiniṉ ṟāṟṟu kiṉṟaṉaḷ kūntal.
2-m tōḻi:	nīrā ṭiṉaḷō innici?
mutaṟṟōḻi: 	ām!ām!
70	eḻuntu vācanī rāṭi muṉcurat
	taḻuntiya aṉṟutāṉ aṇintaveṇ paṭṭiṉik
	koṭuvarap paṇittaṅ katuvē tarittu
	neṭunutal tilakamum nērpaṭat tīṭṭi,
	aṉṟirā aṇintamut tāramum aṇintu,
75	niṉṟutaṉ niliyelām āṭiyil nōkki,
	‘naṉṟō naṅkāy! vāṇi! naviluti!
	aṉṟupōl aṉṟō iṉṟeṉ nilimi!’
	eṉṟu ciṟumuṟu valittaṉaḷ. eṉcola!
	uruvamum uṭiyum uriyum naṭiyum
80	caruvamum pāvaṉi paṇṇiyum...	(aḻa)
		(maṉōṉmaṇiyum vāṇiyum vara)
		aḵtō!
2-m tōḻi:	vantaṉaḷ kāṇuti. vāṇiyum piṉṉuḷaḷ.

			(tōḻimār pōka)
maṉō:		entipōl tayāniti eṅkaṇum illi.
	vantaṉam vaḻaṅkavum vāykū cuvatē!
85	ētō oruvitam eḻutiṉēṉ eṉka!
	vāṇi!uṉ maṇattiṟ kicintāṉ maṉṉaṉ.
	kāṇā yītō ataṟkuḷa kaṭṭaḷi.

(koccakak kalippā)
nīrniliyiṉ mutaliyiṉvāy nilikulinta orukarimuṉ
ōr muṟiyuṉ peyar viḷikka utaviṉivan teṉavurippar;
ārtuyara aḷakkarviḻum aṟiviliyāṉ aḻippataṟkuṉ
pērteriyēṉ āyiṭiṉum piṟakiṭalniṉ peruntakiyō.	1
pāraracar tukiluriyap paritavikkum oruterivi
cīrtuvari nakarkarutic citivoḻintāḷ eṉurippar;
ārtuṇiyum aṟavirukkum aṟiviliyāṉ aḻippataṟkuṉ
ūrteriyēṉ āyiṭiṉum uṟutitaral uṉakkurittē.	2
maṟalivara maṉampataṟum mārkkaṇṭaṉ uṉatiliṅkak
kuṟitaḻuvi aḻivilvaram koṇṭāṉmuṉ eṉavurippar;
veṟikaḻumip poṟiyaḻiyum vempāvi viravutaṟkuṉ
neṟiyaṟiyēṉ āyiṭiṉum nērniṟṟal niṉataruḷē.	3

	uṉṉaṉ puṇmiyēl iṉṉamum kāppaṉ.

3-m paṭi:		ūṟṟirun toḻuki uḷvaṟan tatukaṇ.

	curuṅkiyiṉ taṉmi colli yeṉṉiyiṅ
205	koruṅkē aḻittāṉ uṉṉakar kavara.
	uṉṉara curimiyum uṉṉakar nāṭum
	eṉṉiṭam irantāṉ iccū titaṟkā!

	cūtum turōkamum coliuṉit teruṭṭa

	aṇipeṟu moḻiyiṉ aruttamum pōla,
	annici yākaveñ ñāṉṟum

(kalittuṟi)
ciṟitā yiṉumpaṟ ṟilātuki yaṟṟa tirumakaṭaṉ
kuṟiyān talivaṉ kuṭilaṉpiṉ eytiya koḷkikaṇṭīr
aṟivōm eṉunam akaṅkaram āṟum avattiyiṉiṟ
ceṟivā yirukkun tirukku veḷippaṭum cīrituvē.

aintām aṅkam muṟṟiṟṟu.

maṉōṉmaṇīyam muṟṟiṟṟu.

purāṇakkati viḷakkam
akattiyar kaṭalkuṭitta kati
pakkam 17
orukālattil mittirar varuṇar eṉpavarkaḷ pēraḻakiyākiya ūrvaciyik kaṇṭu avaḷ mītu ācikoṇṭārkaḷ. ataṉāl avarkaḷukku vintu veḷippaṭṭatu. oruvar vintu kuṭattilum, maṟṟoruvar vintu nīrilum viḻuntaṉa. kuṭattil viḻuntatiliruntu akattiyarum. nīril viḻuntatiliruntu vaciṣṭarum uṇṭāṉārkaḷ. kuṭattiliruntu uṇṭāṉapaṭiyiṉālē akattiyarukkuk kuṭamuṉi eṉṟu peyar ēṟpaṭṭatu.
tēvarkaḷukkum acurarkaḷukkum orukālattil pōr ēṟpaṭṭatu. viruttirācuraṉum ēṉiya acurarkaḷum kaṭaliṉ uḷḷē pukuntu oḷintukoṇṭaṉar. appōtu intiraṉ akattiya muṉivariṭam vantu acurarkaḷi veḷippaṭuttavēṇṭum eṉṟu vēṇṭikkoḷḷa, ataṟku avar uṭaṉpaṭṭuk kaṭalnīrik kiyiṉāl mukantu aruntiṉar. ataṉāl kaṭalnīr vaṟṟiyatu. appōtu aṅku maṟintirunta virut tirācuraṉ  mutaliya acurarkaḷi intiraṉ koṉṟāṉ. piṟaku, intiraṉ vēṇṭikkoṇṭapaṭi akattiyar mīṇṭum nīrik kaṭalil umiḻntār.
	“	muṉikkaracu kiyāl mukantu, muḻaṅkum
paṉikkaṭalum, uṇṇap paṭum.”
cakarar cākaram tōṇṭiya kati
parata kaṇṭatti aracāṇṭa cakara cakkaravartti oru kālattil acuva mēta yākam ceyyat toṭaṅkiṉār. yākam ceyvataṟku muṉṉar, yākak kutiriyit tēcam eṅkum cuṟṟi valamvarumpaṭi aṉuppukiṟa vaḻakkampōla, tamatu kutiriyi aṉuppiṉār. poṟāmi koṇṭa intiraṉ. oruvarum aṟiyāmal yākak kutiriyik koṇṭupōyp pātāḷa lōkattilē tavam ceytukoṇṭirunta kapila muṉivariṉ arukil kaṭṭiviṭṭuc ceṉṟāṉ. yākak kutiri ulakameṅkum tēṭiyum kāṇāmiyāl, cakara cakkaravarttiyiṉ piḷḷikaḷ aṟupati ṉāyiravarum pātāḷa lōkattiṟ ceṉṟu tēṭalām eṉṟu eṇṇi parata kaṇṭattiṉ kiḻakkup pakkattit tōṇṭikkoṇṭu pātāḷam ceṉṟārkaḷ. kutiriyik kaṇṭārkaḷ. ivarkaḷ tōṇṭiya perum paḷḷam nīr niṟintu kaṭalāyiṟṟu. cakara cakkaravarttiyiṉ aṟupati ṉāyiram piḷḷikaḷukkum cakarar (cakara cakkaravarttiyiṉ piḷḷikaḷ) eṉpatu potuppeyar. cakarar tōṇṭiya paṭiyiṉāl kīḻkkaṭal cākaram eṉṟu peyarpeṟṟatu.
civaperumāṉ - nakkīrar kati
caṇpakamāṟaṉ eṉṉum pāṇṭiyaṉuṭiya maṉiviyiṉ kūntalil naṟumaṇam vīciṟṟu. pāṇṭiyaṉ, inta naṟumaṇam kūntalil iyaṟkiyāka uṇṭāyiṟṟō. ceyaṟkiyāka amintatō eṉṟu aiyuṟṟāṉ. taṉatu aiyattit tīrppavarukku āyiram poṉ vekumati yaḷippatākak kūṟi, tamiḻc caṅka maṇṭapattil poṟkiḻiyit toṅka viṭṭāṉ.
appoḻutu ālavāyk kōvilil aruccaṉi ceyyum tarumi, maṇampuriyap paṇam illāmal iruntavaṉ civaperumāṉiṭam vēṇṭikkoḷḷa, avar ‘koṅkutēr vāḻkki añciṟit tumpi’ eṉṟu toṭaṅkum oru ceyyuḷi iyaṟṟi ataṉik koṇṭu pōyc caṅkattāriṭam paṭittukkāṭṭip poṟkiḻiyip peṟṟuk koḷḷum paṭi kūṟiṉār. (kuṟuntoki 2-ām ceyyuḷik kāṇka.)  tarumi avvāṟē caṅkattāriṭam ceṉṟu ac ceyyuḷip paṭittuk kāṭṭip poṟkiḻiyik kēṭṭāṉ. capiyilirunta nakkīrar, “ic ceyyuḷil makaḷir kūntalukku iyaṟki maṇam uṇṭu eṉṟu colliyiruppataṉāl itu kuṟṟamuḷḷa ceyyuḷ” eṉṟu ac ceyyuḷil kuṟṟam kaṇṭu kūṟiṉār.
atik kēṭṭa tarumi mīṇṭum kōvilukkuc ceṉṟu civaperu māṉiṭam naṭantatik kūṟiṉāṉ. civaperumāṉ pulavar uruvattōṭu caṅka maṇṭapattiṟku vantār. vantu, “eṉ ceyyuḷil kuṟṟam kaṇṭavaṉ yār?” eṉṟu kēṭṭār. nakkīrar, tāṉ kuṟṟam kaṇṭatākak kūṟiṉār. “eṉṉa kuṟṟam?” eṉṟu civaṉ kēṭka, avar muṉpu kūṟiyatu pōla, “makaḷirkkuk kūntalil iyaṟki maṇam uṇṭeṉpatu kuṟṟam” eṉṟu kūṟiṉār. civaperumāṉ ataṟku viṭi kūṟa muṭiyāmal, tamatu neṟṟik kaṇṇit tiṟantu kāṭṭiṉār. nakkīrar ataṟkum añcāmal,  “neṟṟik kaṇṇik kāṭṭiṉālum kuṟṟam kuṟṟamē!” eṉṟār.
tiruñāṉacampantariṉ puṉalvātam
tiruñāṉacampantar maturikkuc ceṉṟapōtu pāṇṭiyaṉ capiyilē avarukkum camaṇarkaḷukkum camayavātam nikaḻntatu. atil puṉalvātamum oṉṟu. iru camayattārum taṅkaḷ matak karutti eḻutiya paṉiyēṭṭi vikiyāṟṟil viṭuvatu eṉṟum, yāruṭiya ēṭu āṟṟukku etirākac ceṉṟu kariyēṟukiṟatō avaruṭiya camayamē meyc camayam eṉṟu oppukkoḷḷappaṭum eṉṟum muṭivu ceyyappaṭṭatu. avvāṟē tiruñāṉacampantarum camaṇarum tattam matak koḷkiyiṉi eḻutiya paṉiyēṭṭi vikiyāṟṟil viṭṭaṉar. camaṇar eḻuti viṭṭa ēṭu āṟṟu veḷḷattil aṭittuc ceṉṟatu. tiruñāṉacampantar viṭṭa ēṭu veḷḷattiṟku etirākac ceṉṟu kari otuṅkiṟṟu.
imayamum potikiyum camamāṉa kati
pakkam17 
orukālattil civaperumāṉ maliyaracaṉ makaḷākiya pārvatiyit  tirumaṇam ceytār. at tirumaṇam imayamaliyil naṭipeṟṟatu. at tirumaṇattik kāṇat tēvarum maṉitarum ēṉi yōrum imayamalikkuc ceṉṟaṉar. ataṉāl imayamali kaṉatti ṉāl tāḻntu, potikimali uyarntatu. atukaṇṭa civaperumāṉ, akattiya muṉivarip potikimalikkup pōkumpaṭi kūṟiṉār. ataṉ paṭiyē akattiyar vantu potikimaliyil taṅkiṉār. appōtu, paḷuvillātapaṭiyāl mēleḻunta potiki mali tāḻntu, imayamum potikiyum camamāka niṉṟaṉa.
cannu kātil kaṅki veḷippaṭṭa kati
pakkam 18
pakīrataṉ eṉpavaṉ neṭuṅkālam tavam iruntu ākāya kaṅkiyip pūlōkattil koṇṭu vantāṉ. pūmiyil vanta kaṅki pāyntu ōṭiyatu. vaḻiyil cannu muṉivar yākam ceytu koṇṭirun tār.  avar, kaṅkiyāṟu ōṭivaruvatik kaṇṭu. atu yāka kuṇṭattil pukuntu yākat tīyi aḻittuviṭum eṉṟu añci. kaṅkinīr muḻuvatiyum kuṭittuviṭṭār. appōtu pakīrataṉum, tēvarkaḷum muṉivarkaḷum kaṅkiyāṟṟi veḷiyē viṭumpaṭi cannuvi vēṇṭikkoṇṭaṉar. ivarkaḷiṉ vēṇṭukōḷukku iṇaṅki cannu muṉivar. kaṅkiyāṟṟit tamatu kātiṉ vaḻiyāka veḷiyē viṭṭār. itaṉāl, kaṅki yāṟṟukkuc cānuvi eṉṉum peyar ēṟpaṭṭatu.
kākki kāviriyi uṇṭākkiya kati
pakkam 18. vāyacam kaviḻtta poṉṉi
intiraṉ pūñcōli amikka virumpiṉāṉ. cōlikku nīr vēṇṭumākiyāl, avaṉ vināyakak kaṭavuḷi vaḻipaṭṭāṉ. avvamayam potiki maliyi nōkkip pōykkoṇṭirunta akattiya muṉivar, kamaṇṭalattik kīḻē vittapōtu vināyakar kākki vaṭivamākac ceṉṟu kamaṇṭala nīrik kaviḻttuviṭṭār. annīr perukik kāviriyāṟākap pāyntatu.
maṉmataṉi eritta kati 
pakkam 22
tārakācuraṉi aḻippataṟkāka murukakkaṭavuḷi utavavēṇṭum eṉṟu tēvarkaḷ civaperumāṉi vēṇṭiṉārkaḷ. appōtu civaperumāṉ yōkattil amarntiruntār. yōka niṣṭiyik kalittup pārvati mītu icci koḷḷumpaṭic ceyya tēvarkaḷ maṉmataṉi vēṇṭiṉārkaḷ. maṉmataṉ ceṉṟu malarkkaṇikaḷic civaperumāṉmītu eṟintāṉ. avar neṟṟik kaṇṇit tiṟantu pārttār. maṉmataṉ erintu cāmpalāṉāṉ. maṉmataṉ maṉivi irati taṉ kaṇavaṉip piḻikkumpaṭi irantu vēṇṭiṉāḷ. civaperumāṉ, maṉmataṉi uyirppittu eḻuppi, iratikku maṭṭum teriyumpaṭiyum maṟṟavarkaḷukkuk kāṇappaṭāta paṭiyum amittār. ākavē, maṉmataṉ aṉaṅkaṉ (uruvam illātavaṉ) eṉṟu peyar peṟṟāṉ. avaṉ aintuvitamāṉa pū ampukaḷi eytu ciṟṟiṉpa uṇarcciyi maṉitar uḷḷattil ūṭṭukiṟāṉ eṉpatu purāṇak kati.
kaṭuvuṇṭa kaṇṭar
pakkam 22
tēvarkaḷum acurarkaḷum pāṟkaṭalik kaṭintārkaḷ. appōtu ak kaṭaliliruntu cila poruḷkaḷ tōṉṟiṉa. avaṟṟuṭaṉ nañcum tōṉṟiyatu. nañcik kaṇṭavuṭaṉ avarkaḷ añci ōṭiṉārkaḷ. civaperumāṉiṭam ceṉṟu pāṟkaṭalil nañcu veḷippaṭṭatik kūṟiṉārkaḷ. civaperumāṉ, ālāla cuntarari aṉuppi nañcik koṇṭuvarac ceytu ataṉit tamatu vāyil iṭṭu viḻuṅkiṉār.  viḻuṅkumpōtu pārvatiyār, nañcu uḷḷē pōkātapaṭi avar kaḻuttil niṟuttiṉār. itaṉāl civaperumāṉukkuk kaṭuvuṇṭa kaṇṭāṉ (kaṭu - viṣam) eṉṟum, nañcuṇṭa kaṇṭaṉ eṉṟum, kāḷa kaṇṭaṉ, kaṟik kaṇṭaṉ, nīlakaṇṭaṉ eṉṟum peyarkaḷ ēṟpaṭṭaṉa. 
ariccantiraṉ kati
pakkam 31
poy collā viratattōṭu vāḻnta ariccantiraṉip poyyaṉākka vicuvāmittirar avaṉukkup pala tuṉpaṅkaḷ ēṟpaṭac ceykiṟār.  ariccantiraṉiṭam perumporuḷi yākattiṉ poruṭṭu yācittup peṟṟukkoṇṭu, apporuḷi avaṉiṭamē vittup pōkiṟār. piṟaku nāṭṭiyap peṇkaḷi aṉuppi aracaṉiṭamiruntu apporuḷkaḷit tāṉamākap peṟumpaṭi ceykiṟār. poruḷ illāta camayattil vantu kēṭṭu āṭciyip peṟṟuk koḷkiṟār. ivvāṟu poruḷiyum araciyum iḻanta ariccantiraṉ kaṭiciyil taṉṉiyum taṉ maṉiviyiyum makaṉiyum viṟkumpaṭiyāṉa ikkaṭṭāṉa   niliyaṭiyumpaṭi ceykiṟār. kaṭiciyākat tuṉpaṅkaḷ nīṅkiṉār. cattiyamē vellum eṉpati niṟuviṉār.
intiraṉ urukkaranta kati 
pakkam 31
ciyavaṉa muṉivar intiraṉukku avirppākam koṭukkāmal, acuviṉi tēvarkaḷukkuk koṭuppatik kaṇṭu intiraṉ am muṉivar mēlē vaccirāyutatti eṟintāṉ. avar avaṉic capittār.  ataṉāl avaṉ kaṭalil oḷintāṉ eṉpatu oru kati. 
turuvāca muṉivar tēvēntiraṉukku  oru malarik koṭukka avaṉ ati vāṅkit tāṉ ēṟiyirunta airāvatayāṉiyiṉ taliyil vittāṉ. anta yāṉi antap pūvit tumpikkiyiṉāl eṭuttut taṉ kālil vittut tēyttatu. atukaṇṭu ciṉanta turuvāca muṉivar avaṉi vēṭaṉākumpaṭi capittār. intiraṉ ac cāpattiṉāl kāṭṭil vēṭaṉākat tirintāṉ eṉpatu maṟṟoru kati.
intiraṉ akalikiyik kaṟpaḻitta kāraṇattiṉāl, kucika muṉivar avaṉ uṭampu muḻuvatum peṇkuṟiyākak kaṭavatu eṉṟu capikka, ac cāpam ēṟṟa intiraṉ veṭki veḷiyē varamuṭiyāmal maṟintu vāḻntāṉ eṉpatu iṉṉoru kati.
kaṭalil amutam tōṉṟiya kati 
pakkam 97
tēvarkaḷum acurarkaḷum pāṟkaṭalik kaṭintārkaḷ. mēru maliyi mattākavum vācuki eṉṉum pāmpi mattik kaṭiyum kayiṟākavum koṇṭu tēvarkaḷ orupuṟamum  acurarkaḷ orupuṟamum iruntu kaṭintapōtu, ap pāṟkaṭaliliruntu amutam tōṉṟiyatu.  amutam eṭukkat tēvarkaḷōṭu uḻitta acurarkaḷukku uriya pākattik koṭukkāmal, tēvarkaḷ vañcaṉiyāka ati eṭuttuk koṇṭu pōyt tāṅkaḷ maṭṭum aruntiṉārkaḷ.  amutatti aruntiyapaṭiyāl nōyiṉṟi neṭuṅkālam vāḻntārkaḷ.
 pāmpu cantiraṉi viḻuṅkiya kati 
pakkam 126
pāṟkaṭalik kaṭintapōtu atiliruntu amutam kiṭittatu. anta amutattit tammuṭaṉ uḻitta acurarkaḷukkup paṅku koṭukkāmal koṇṭupōyt tēvarkaḷ maṭṭum pakirntu cāppiṭ ṭārkaḷ. appōtu irāku kētu eṉṉum iraṇṭu acurarkaḷ tēvarkaḷ pōla uruvam koṇṭu  tēvarkaḷ amutam cāppiṭukiṟa pantiyil pōy uṭkārntārkaḷ. itiyaṟinta cūriyaṉ cantiraṉākiya tēvarkaḷ. amutattip paṅkiṭukiṟa viṣṇuviṉiṭam kuṟippākat terivittār kaḷ. viṣṇu avarkaḷi aṭittut turattiṉār.  ataṉāl, cūriya cantirarkaḷiṉmēl pakikoṇṭu irāku kētukkaḷākiya acurarkaḷ pāmpiṉ uruvaṅ koṇṭu avarkaḷi viḻuṅkuvatākavum. appaṭi viḻuṅkuvataṉāltāṉ cūriya kirakaṇam, cantira kirakaṇam ēṟpaṭukiṉṟaṉa eṉṟum purāṇak kati kūṟukiṟatu.
kajēntira mōkṣam 
pakkam 291
kajēntiraṉ eṉṉum yāṉi tirumāl mītu paktikoṇṭu, nāḷ tōṟum nīrōṭiyil tāmarippūvip paṟittup perumāḷip pūcittukkoṇṭiruntatu.  orunāḷ oru mutali yāṉiyiṉ kālip piṭittu nīril iḻuttatu. yāṉiyum palaṅkoṇṭa maṭṭum mutaliyik karikku iḻuttatu. ataṉāl  muṭiyavilli. mutali yāṉiyi nīril iḻuttuc ceṉṟatu. avvamayam, yāṉi tirumāli niṉittatu. tirumāl vantu tamatu kiccakkarattiṉāl mutaliyik koṉṟu yāṉiyi viṭuvittu, ataṟku mōkṣam tantaruḷiṉār. 
tukilurinta kati 
pakkam 291
pāṇṭavar aivarum kuravaruṭaṉ cūtāṭi nāṭu nakaram mutaliyavikaḷit tōṟṟaṉar.  kaṭiciyil, tarumaputtirar, tirupati yiyum paṇiyam vittuc cūtāṭiṉār. avaḷiyum tōṟṟār. appōtu, turiyōtaṉaṉ tirupatiyic capiyil aḻittu avaḷ tukili aviḻttu māṉapaṅkam ceytāṉ. tirupatikku utavi ceyvār oruvarumilar. appōtu tirupati, kaṇṇapirāṉit tiyāṉam ceytāḷ.  kaṇṇaṉ tiruvaruḷāl avaḷ aṇintirunta āṭi vaḷarntukoṇṭēyiruntatu. ivvāṟu kaṇṇapirāṉ tiruvaruḷiṉāl tirupati māṉam kāppāṟṟappaṭṭāḷ.
mārkkaṇṭēyaṉ kati
pakkam 291
mārkkaṇṭēyaṉ eṉpavar naṟkuṇamum nalloḻukkamum uṭiyavarāyiruntār. āṉāl, avarukku aṟpāyuḷ eṉpatu aṟintu avaruṭiya peṟṟōrkaḷ maṉam kavaṉṟārkaḷ. mārkkaṇṭēyar, tamatu peṟṟōriṉ maṉa varuttattit tīrkkumporuṭṭuc civaliṅka vaḻipāṭu ceytukoṇṭiruntār. avarukkup patiṉāṟu vayatāṉa pōtu, avar uyirik kavara iyamaṉ vantāṉ. mārkkaṇṭēyar tām pūciceytukoṇṭirunta civaliṅkattik kaṭṭit taḻuvik koṇṭār. iyamaṉ pācakkayiṟṟi eṟintu avar uyirik kavara muyaṟci ceytāṉ. appōtu civaperumāṉ veḷippaṭṭu yamaṉi utittut taḷḷi mārkkaṇṭēyarukku eṉṟum patiṉāṟuvayatu tantaruḷiṉār eṉpatu purāṇak kati.

THE SECRET WAY
By The Rt. Hon. Lord Lytton
The very striking legend which suggests the following poem is found in Atheneus, book xiii. c. 35. It is there given as a quotation from the History of Alexander, by Chares of Mitylene.’ The author adds, that ‘ the story is often told by the barbarians who dwell ia Asia, and is exceedingly admired; and they have pinted representations of the story in their temples and palaces, and also in their private houses.’ In constructing the plot of the poem, I have? made some variations in incident and denouement from the meagre outlines of the old romance preserved in Atheneus, with a view of heightening the interest which springs from the groundwork of the legend. I should add that the name of the Scythian king’s dughter is changed from Odatis, which, for narrative purpose, a little too nearly resembles that of her father, Omartes-to Argiope : a name more Hellenic it is true, but it may be reasonably doubted whether that of Odatis be more genuinely Scythian. For the sake of euphony, the name of the Persian Prince is softened from Zariadres to Zariades. This personage is sid by the author whom Atheneus quotes, to have been the brother of Hystaspes, and to have held dominion over the country from above the Caspian Gates to the river Tanis (the modern Don). Assuming that he existed historically, and was the brother of Hystaspes and uncle to Darius I, he would have held the dominions assigned to him, as a satrap under Cambyses, not as an independent sovereign. But in a romance of this kind, it would be hypercritical, indeed, to demand strict historical accuracy. Although . the hero of the legend would have been, as described, of purely Persian origin (a royal Achemenian), and the people subjected to him would not have belonged to Media proper, in the poem he is sometimes called the Mede, and his people Medes, according to an usage sufficiently common among Greek writers when speaking generally of the rulers and people of the great Persian Empire. It may scarcely be worth while to observe that though in subsequent tales where the Hellenic deities are more or less prominently introduced or referred to, their Hellenic names are assigned to them, yet in the passing allusions made in this poem to the God of War or the Goddess of Morning, it was judged more agreeable to the general reader to designate those deities by the familiar names of Mars and Aurora, rather than by the Greek appellations of Ares and Eos.
MARTES, King of the wide plins which, north , 
Of Tanis, pasture steeds for Scythian Mars, 
	Forsook the simple ways
		And Nomad tents of his unconquered fathers;	4
And in the fashion of the neighbouring Medes,
Built a great city girt with moat and wall, 
	And in the midst thereof
		A regal palace dwarfing piles in Susa	8
With vast foundations rooted into earth,
And crested summits soaring into Heaven,
	And gates of triple brass, 
		Siege - proof as portals welded by the Cyclops. 	12
One day Omartes, in his pride of heart,
Led his High Priest, Teleutias, thro’ his halls,
	And chilled by frigid looks, 
		When counting on warm prise, asked - ‘What is wanting? 	14
‘Where is beheld the palace of a king,
So stored with all that doth a king beseem;
	The woofs of Phrygian looms,
		The gold of Colchis, and the pearls of Ormus,         	20
‘Couches of ivory sent from farthest Ind,
Sidonian crystal, and Corinthian Bronze,
	Egypt’s vast symbol gods,
		And those imagined into men by Hellas;	24
‘Stored not in tents that tremble to a gale,
But chambers firm - based, as the Pyramids,
	And breaking into spray
		The surge of Time, as Gades breaks the ocean?’      	28
Nor thou nor I the worth of these things now
Can judge ; we stand too near them; sid the sage. 
	None till they reach the tomb
		Scan with just eye the treasures of the palace. 	32
But for the building - as we speak, I feel 
Thro’ all the crannies pierce an icy wind 
	More bitter than the blasts 
		Which howled without the tents of thy rude fathers. 	36
Thou hast forgot to bid thy masons close 
The chinks of stone aginst Calamity.’ 
	The sage inclined his brow, 
		Shivered, and, parting, round him wrapt his mantle. 	40
The king turned, thoughtful, to a favourite chief, 
The rudest champion of the polished change 
	That fixed the wain - borne homes 
		Of the wild Scythian, and encamped a city; 	44
‘Heard’st thou the Sage, brave Seuthes ?’ asked the King. 
Yea, the priest deemed thy treasures insecure, 
	And fain would see them safe In his own temple;’ 
		The King smiled on Seuthes.    	48
Unto this Scythian monarch’s nuptial bed 
	But one fair girl, Argiope was born: 
	For whom no earthly throne 
		Soared from the level of his fond ambition. 	52
To her, indeed, had Aphrodite given 
	Beauty, that royalty which subjects kings, 
	Sweet with unconscious charm, 
		And modest as the youngest of the Graces. 	56
Men blest her when she moved before their eyes 
	Shame - faced, as blushing to be born so fair,
	Mild as that child of gods
		Violet - crowned Athens hallowing named ‘Pity,’*	60
Now, of a sudden, over that bright face 
	There fell the shadow of some troubled thought,
	As cloud, from purest dews
		Updrawn, makes sorrowful a star in heaven: 	64 
And as a nightingale that having heard 
	A perfect music from some master’s lyre,
	Steals into coverts lone, 
		With her own - melodies no more contented,	68
But hunted by the strin, till then unknown, 
	Seeks to re - sing it back, herself to charm.
	Seeks still and ever fails,
		Missing the key - note which unlocks the music,-	72 
So, from her former pastimes in the choir 
	Of comrade virgins, stole Argiope,
	Lone amid summer leaves
		Brooding that thought which was her joy and trouble. 	76
The King discerned the change in his fair child, 
	And questioned oft, yet could not learn the cuse; 
	The sunny bridge between
		The lip and heart which childhood builds was broken. 	80
Not more Aurora, stealing into heaven, 
	Conceals the mystic treasures of the deep
	Whence with chaste blush she comes,
		Than virgin bosoms guard their earliest secret.	84
Omartes sought the priest, to whose wise heart			So dear the miden, he was wont to say
	That grins of crackling salt 
	From her pure hand, upon the altar sprinkled,         	88
Sent up a flame to loftier heights in heaven
	Than that which rolled from hecatombs in smoke.
	‘King,’ sid the musing seer, 
		‘Behold, the woodbine, opening infant blossoms,     	92
‘Perfumes the bank whose herbage hems it round,
	From its own birthplace drinking in delight;
	Later, its instinct stirs; 
		Fain would it climb - to climb forbidden, creepeth,  	96
‘Its lot obeys its yearning to entwine;
	Around the oak it weaves a world of flowers;
	Or, listless drooping, trils 
		Dejected tendrils lost mid weed and briar. 	100
‘There needs no construing to my parable:
	As is the woodbine’s, so the woman’s life:
	Look round the forest kings, 
		And to the stateliest wed thy royal blossom.’         	104
Sharp is a father’s pang when comes the hour
	In which his love contents his child no more,
	And the sweet wonted smile 
		Fades from his hearthstone to rejoice a stranger’s.   	108
But soon from parent love dies thought of self; 
	Omartes, looking round the Lords of earth,
	In young Zariades
		Singled the worthiest of his peerless dughter;       	112
Scion of that illustrious hero - stem, 
	Which in great cyrus bore the loftiest flower 
	Purpled by Orient suns; 
		Stretched his vast satrapies, engulphing kingdoms, 	116
From tranquil palmgroves fringing Caspian waves,
	To the bleak marge of stormy Tanis; 
	On Scythia bordering thus, 
		No foe so dread, and no ally so potent 	120
Perilous boundary - rights by Media climed 
	O’er that great stream which, laving Scythian plins,
	Europe from Asia guards, 
		The Persian Prince, in wedding Scythia’s dughter	 124
Might well resign, in pledge of lasting peace 
	But ill the project of Omartes pleased 
	His- warlike free - born chiefs,
		And ill the wilder tribes of his fierce people; 	128
For Scyth and Mede had long been as those winds
	Whose very meeting in itself is storm,
	Yet the King’s will previled,
		Confirmed, when wavering, by his trusted Seuthes.	132
He, the fierce leader of the fiercest horde,
	Won from the wild by greed of gin and power,
	Stood on the bound between
		Man social and man savage, dark and massive :     	136
So rugged was he that men deemed him true,
	So secret was he that men deemed him wise,
	And he had grown so great,
		The throne was lost behind the subject’s shadow.	140
In the advice he whispered to the king 
	He lid the key - stone of ambitious hope,
	This marriage with the Mede
		Would leave to heirs remote the Scythian kingdom, 	144 
Sow in men’s minds vague fears of foreign rule,
	Which might, if cultured, spring to armed revolt.
	In armed revolt how oft 
		Kings disappear, and none dare call it murder.       	148
And when a crown falls bloodstined in the dust,
	The strong man standing nearest to its fall 
	Takes it and crowns himself; 
		And heirs remote are swept from earth as rebels.     	152
Of peace and marriage-rites thus dreamed the king;
	Of graves and thrones the tritor ; while the fume 
	From altars, loud with prayer 
		To speed the Scythian envoys, darkened heaven.     	156
A hardy prince was young Zariades,
	Scorning the luxuries of the loose - robed Mede,
	Cast in the antique mould 
		Of men whose teaching thewed the sould of Cyrus. 	160
To ride, to draw the bow, to speak the truth, 
	Sufficed to Cyrus,’ sid the prince, when child. 
	‘Astyages knew more’
		Answered the Magi - ‘Yes, and lost his kingdoms.’ 	164
Yet there was in this prince the eager mind 
	Which needs must think, and therefore needs must learn; 
	Natures, whose roots strike deep, 
		Clear their own way, and win to light in growing.    	168
His that rare beauty which both charms and awes
	The popular eye ; his the life-gladdening smile;
	His the death - dooming frown; 
		That which he would he could; men loved and feared him. 172
Now of a sudden over this grand brow
	There fell the gloom of some unquiet thought, 
	As when the south wind sweeps 
		Sunshine from Hadria in a noon of summer: 	176
And as a stag, supreme among the herd, 
	With lifted crest inhaling lusty air,
	Smit by a shaft from far,
		Deserts his lordly range amidst the pasture, 	180
And thro’ dim woodlands with drooped antlers creeps
	To the cool marge of rush-grown watersprings; 
	So from all former sports, 
		Contest, or converse with once-loved companions, 	184
Stole the young prince thro’ unfrequented groves, 
	To gaze with listless eyes on lonely streams. 
	All, wondering, marked the change, 
		None dared to question; he had no fond father.    	188
Now, in the thick of this his altered mood, 
	Arrived the envoys of the Scythian king. 
	Reluctant audience found, 
		And spoke to ears displeased their sovereign’s message.	192
‘Omartes greets Zariades the Mede:
	Between the realms of both there rolls a river 
	Inviolate to the Scyth, 
		Free to no keels but those the Scythian charters:	196
Yet have thy subjects outraged oft its waves.
	And pirate foray on our northern banks 
	Ravaged the flocks and herds, 
		Till Scythian riders ask “Why sleeps the Ruler?”	200
‘Still, loth to fan the sparks which leap to flame
	Reddening the nations, from the breath of kings;
	We have not sought thy throne 
		With tales of injury or appeals to justice; 	204
‘But searching in our inmost heart to find 
	The gentlest bond wherewith to link our realms, 
	Make scyth and Mede akin, 
		By household ties their royal chiefs uniting, 	208
We strip our crown of its most precious gem, 
	Proffering to thee our child Argiope:
	So let the Median Queen 
		Be the mild guardian of the Scythian river. 	211
Lifting his brow, replied Zariades:
	Great rivers are the highways of the world: 
	The Tanis laves my shores;
		For those who dwell upon my shores I clim it.      	216
If pirates land on either side for prey,
	My banks grow herdsmen who can guard their herds;
	Take, in these words, reply
		To all complints that threaten Median subjects.  	  220
But for the gentler phrase wherewith your king 
	Stoops to a proffer, yet implies command, 
	I pray you, in return, 
		To give such thanks as soften most refusal. 	224
‘Thanks are a language kings are born to hear,
	But speak not glibly till they near their fall,
	To guard his Scythian realm,
		On the Mede’s throne the Scyth would place his dughter;	228
‘I should deceive him if I sid “Agreed.:”
	No throne, methinks, hath room for  more than one;
	Where a Queen’s lips decide
		or peace or war, she slays the king her husband.	232
‘Thus thinking, did I wed this Scythian mid,
	It were no marriage between Mede and Scyth;
	Nor wrong I unseen charms;
		Love, we are told, comes like the wind from heaven.	236
‘Not at our bidding, but its won free will.
	And so depart - and pardon my plin speech.
	That which I think I say,
		Offending oft - times, but deceiving never’.	240.
So he dismissed them, if with churlish words,
	With royal present, and to festal pomps,
	But one, by Median law
		Nearest his throne, the chief priest of the Magi,	244
Having heard all with not unprescient fears,
	Followed the Prince and urged recall of words
	Which, sent from king to king,
		Are frught with dragon seeds, whose growth is armies.	248
Mute, as if musing in himself, the Prince
	Heard the wise counsel to its warning close.
	Then, with a gloomy look,
		Gazed on the reader of the stars, and answered	252
Leave thou to me that which to me belongs; 
	My people need the Tanis for their rafts; 
	Or soon or late that need 
		Strings the Mede’s bow, and mounts the Scythian rider. 	256
Mage, I would pluck my spirit from the hold 
	Of a strong phantasy, which, night and day, 
	Haunts it, unsinews life, 
		And makes my heart the foe of my own reason.     	260
Perchance in war, the gods ordin my cure; 
	And courting war, I to myself give peace.
	Startled by these wild words, 
		The Mage, in trust-alluring arts long - practised,         	264
Led on the Prince to unfold their hidden sense; 
	And having bound the listener by the oath 
	Mage never broke, to hold
		Sacred the trust, the King thus told his trouble.     	  268
Know that each night (thro’ three revolving moons) 
	An image comes before me in a dream;
	Ever the same sweet face,
		Lovely as that which blest the Carian’s slumber.    	272
Nought mid the dark - eyed dughters of the East,
	Nought I have ever seen in waking hours,
	Rivals in charm this shape 
		Which hath no life - unless a dream hath substance. 	276
‘But never yet so clearly visible, 
	Nor with such joy in its celestial smile 
	Hath come the visitant, 
		Making a temple of the soul it hallows, 	280
As in the last night’s vision; there it stooped 
	Over my brow, with tresses that I touched.
	With love in bashful eyes, 
		With breath whose fragrance lingered yet in waking, 	284
And balmed the morn, as when a dove, that brings 
	Ambrosia to Olympus, sheds on earth
	Drops from a passing wing: 
		Surely the vision made itself thus living 	288
To test my boast, that truth so fills this soul 
	It could not lodge a falsehood ev’n in dream: 
	Wonderest thou, Magian, now, 
		Why I refuse to wed the Scythian’s dughter ?     	292
And if I thus confide to thee a tale 
	I would not whisper into ears profane, 
	Tis that where reason ends, 
		Men have no choice between the Gods and Chos. 	296
Ye Magi are the readers of the stars, 
	Versed in the language of the world of dreams: 
	Wherefore consult thy lore, 
		And tell me if Earth hold a mortal miden 	300
In whom my nightly vision breathes and moves.
	If not, make mine such talismans and spells,
	As banish from the soul 
		Dreams that annul its longing for the daylight.       	304
Up to his lofty fire-tower climbed the mage,
	Explored the stars and drew Chaldean schemes;
	Thrid the drak maze of books
		Opening on voids beyond the bounds of Nature; 	308
Placed crystal globes in hands of infants pure; 
	Invoked the demonds hunting impious graves; 
	And all, alas, in vin; 
		The dream, adjured aginst itself to witness, 	312
Refused to. wander from the gate of horn, 
	To stars, scrolls, crystals, infants, demons, proof. 
	Foiled of diviner lore 
		The Mage resumed his wisdom as a mortal; 	316
And since no Mage can own his science fails, 
	But where that solves not, still solution finds, 
	So he resought the King, 
		Grave-browed as one whose brin holds Truth new - captured:320
Saying, ‘O King, the shape thy dreams have glassed 
	Is of the Colchian Mother of the Medes; 
	When, on her dragon car, 
		From faithless Jason rose subline Medea, 	324
Refuge at Athens she with Aegeus found; 
	To him espoused she bore one hero - son, 
	Medus, the Sire of Medes; 
		And if that form no earthly shape resembles         	328
What marvel? for her beauty witched the world, 
	Ev’n in an age when woman lured the gods; 
	Retining yet dread powers 
		(For memories die not) of her ancient magic, 	332
Her spirit lingers in these Orient airs, 
	And guards the children of her latest love, 
	Thus, hovering over thee, 
		She warms they heart to love in her - those children. 	336
As in her presence thou didst feel thy soul 
	Lodged in a temple, so the Queen commands 
	That thou restore the fanes 
		And deck the altars where her Medus worshipped; 	340
And in the spirit - breath which balmed the morn 
	Is symbolized the incense on our shrines, 
	Which, as thou renderest here,
		Shall waft thee after death to the Immortals.	344
Seek, then, no talisman aginst the dream, 
	Obey its mandates, and return its love;
	So shall thy reign be blest, 
		And in Zariades revive a Medus.	348
‘Friend,’ sighed the King, ‘albeit I needs must own
	All dreams mean temples, where a Mage explins,
	Yet when a young man dreams
		Of decking altars, ’tis not for Medea,	352
He sid and turned to lose himself in groves,
	Shunning the sun.   In wrath aginst the stars
	The Mage resought his tower.
		And that same day went back the Scythian envoys.	356
But from the night which closed upon that day, 
	The image of the dream began to fade, 
	Fainter and paler seen, 
		With saddened face and outlines veiled in vapour;	360
At last it vanished as a lingering star 
	Fades on Citheron from Maenad’s eyes, 
	Mid cymbal, fife, and horn, 
		When sunrise flashes on the Car of Panthers.	364
As the dream fled, broke war upon the land; 
	The “Scythian hosts had crossed the Tanis. 
	And, where the dreamer dreamed, 
		An angry King surveyed his Asian armies. 	368
Who first in fault, the Scythian or the Mede, 
	Who first broke compact, or transgressed a bound, 
	Historic scrolls dispute  
		As Scyth or Mede interprets dreams in story. 	372
Enough for war when two brave nations touch, 
	With rancour simmering in the hearts of kings:
	War is the child of cloud
		Oftentimes stillest just before the thunder. 	376
The armies met in that vast plin whereon 
	The Chaldee, meting out the earth, became 
	The scholar of the stars, - 
		A tombless plin, yet has it buried empires. 	380
At first the Scythian horsemen, right to left, 
	Broke wings by native Medes outstretched for flight, 
	But in the central host 
		Stood Persia’s sons, the mountin race of Cyrus;   	384
And in their midst, erect in golden car
	With looks of scorn, Zariades the King;
	And at his trumpet voice 
		Steed felt as man that now began the battle. 	388
Up, sons of Persia, Median women fly; 
	And leave the field to us whom gods made men: 
	The Scythian chases well 
	Yon timorous deer; how let him front the lions.    	392
He spoke, and light - touched by his charioteer 
	Rushed his white steeds down the quick - parted lines; 
	The parted lines quick - closed, 
		Following that car as after lightning follow 	396
The hil and whirlwind of collected storm: 
	The Scyths had scattered their own force in chase, 
	As torrents split in rills 
		The giant waves whose gathered might were deluge; 	400
And, as the Scythian strength is in the charge 
	Of its fierce riders, so that charge, misspent, 
	Left weak the ignobler ranks,
		Fighting on foot; alert in rid or skirmish, 	404
And skilled in weapons striking foes from far,
	But all untught to front with levelled spears, 
	And rampart-line of shields, 
		The serried onslught of converging battle:            	408
Wavering, recoiling, turning oft, they fled; 
	Omartes was not with them to uphold;
	Foremost himself had rode 
		Heading the charge by which the Medes were scattered;	412
And when, believing victory won, he turned 
	His bloody reins back to the central war, 
	Behold, - a clud of dust, 
		And thro’ the cloud the ruins of an army!	416
At sunset, sole king on that plin, reigned Death. 
	Far off, the dust-cloud rolled; far off, behind 
	A dust-cloud followed fast; 
		The hunted and the hunter, Flight and Havoc	420
‘With the scant remnant of his mighty host 
	(Many who ‘scaped the foe forsook their chief 
	For plins more safe than walls.) 
		The Scythian King repassed his brazen portals,      	424
In haste he sent to gather fresh recruits 
	Among the fiercest tribes his fathers ruled, 
	They whom a woman led	
		When to her feet they tossed the head of Cyrus.     	428
And the tribes answered - ‘Let the Scythian King 
	Return repentant to old Scythian ways, 
	And la.ugh with us at foes.
		Wains know no sieges - Freedom moves her cities 	432
Soon came the Victor with his Persian guards, 
	And all the rallied vengeance of his Medes; 
	One night, sprang up dread camps 
		With lurid watch-lights circling doomed ramparts, 	436
As hunters round the wild beasts in their lir 
	Marked for the javelin, wind a belt of fire. 
	Omartes scanned his walls 
		And sid, ‘Ten years Troy baffled Agamemnon.    	 440
Yet pile up walls, out-topping Babylon, 
	Manned foot by foot with sleepless sentinels, 
	And to and fro will pass, 
		Free as the air thro keyholes, Love and Treason.  	444
Be elsewhere told the horrors of that siege, 
	The desperate sally, slughter, and repulse; 
	Repelled in turn the foe, 
		With Titan ladders scaling cloud - capt bulwarks,      	448
Hurled back and buried under rocks heaved down 
	By wrathful hands from scatheless battlements.
	With words of holy charm,
		Soothing despir and leaving resignation,	452
Mild thro’ the city moved Argiope, 
	Pale with a sorrow too divine for fear; 
	And when, at morn and eve,
		She bowed her meek head to her father’s blessing,	456
Omartes felt as if the righteous gods 
	Could doom no altars at whose foot she prayed. 
	Only, when all alone,
		Stole from her lips a murmur like complint,	460
Shaped in these words, ‘Wert thou, then, but a dream? 
	Or shall I see thee in the Happy Fields ?
	Now came with stony eye 
		The livid vanquisher of cities. Famine;	464
And moved to pity now, the Persian sent 
	Heralds with proffered peace on terms that seem 
	Gentle to Asian kings, 
		And unendurable to Europe’s Freemen;	468
I from thy city will withdraw ray hosts, 
	And leave thy people to their chiefs and laws, 
	Taking from all thy realm 
		Nought save the river, which I make my border,	472
If but, in homage to my sovereign throne, 
	Thou pay this petty tribute once a year;
	Six grins of Scythian soil, 
		One urn of water spared from Scythian fountins.	476
And the Scyth answered - ‘Let the Mede demand 
	That which is mine to give, or gold or life; 
	The water and the soil 
		Are, every grin and every drop, my country’s:       	480
And no man hath a country where a King, 
	Pays tribute to another for his crown.
	And at this stern reply, 
		The Persian doomed to fire and sword the city.       	484
Omartes stood within his palace hall, 
	And by his side Teleutias, the high priest. 
	‘And rightly,’ sid the King, 
		‘Did thy prophetic mind rebuke vin - glory. 	488
‘Lend me thy mantle now; I feel the wind 
	Pierce through the crannies of the thick-ribbed stone.
	‘No wind lasts long,’ replied, 
		With soothing voice,- the hierarch. Calm and tempest 	492
‘Follow .each other in the outward world, 
	And joy and sorrow in the heart of man:
	Wherefore take comfort now,
		The earth and water of the Scyth are grateful,         	496
‘And as thou hast, inviolate to the Scyth, 
	His country saved, that country yet to thee 
	Stretches out chinless arms, 
		And for these walls gives plins that mock besiegers, 	500
Traversed by no invader save the storm, 
	Nor girt by watchfires nearer than the stars. 
	Beneath these regal halls,
		Know that there lies a road which leads to safety. 	504
‘For, not unpresicent of the present ills 
	When rose thy towers, the neighbours of the cloud, 
	I, like the mole, beneath, 
		Worked path secure aginst cloud - riving thunder.   	508
‘Employing Aethiops skilled not in our tongue, 
	Held day and night in the dark pass they hewed:
	And the work done, sent home:
		So the dumb earthworm shares alone the secret.     	512
‘Lo, upon one side ends the unguessed road
	There, - its door panelled in yon far recess,
	Where, on great days of state,
		Oft has thy throne been set beneath the purple;      	5l6
‘The outward issue opes beyond the camp,
	Mid funeral earth mounds, skirting widths of plin, 
	Where graze the fleetest steeds, 
		And rove the bravest riders Scythia nurtures,         	520
They whom thou ne’er could’st lure to walls of stone, 
	Nor rouse to war, save for their own free soil.
	These gined, defy the foe;
		Let him pursue and space itself engulphs him.	524
Omartes answered - ‘With the towers I built 
	Must I, O Kind adviser, stand or fall.
	Kings are not merely men -
		Epochs their lives, their actions the world’s story.	528
I sought to wean my people from the wild, 
	To centre scattered valours, wasted thoughts,
	Into one mind, a State;
		Failing in this, my life as king has perished:	532
And as mere man I should disdin to live. 
	Deemest thou now I could go back content
	A Scytb among the scyths ?
		I am no eaglet - I have borne the aegis. 	536
‘But life, as life, suffices youth for joy.
	Young plants win sunbeams, shift them as we may.
	So to the Nomad tribes
		Lead thou their Queen. - O sa”ve, ye gods, my dughter!’ 	540
The king’s proud head bowed o’er the hierarch’s breast. 
	‘Not unto me confide that precious charge,’
	Replied the sweet voiced seer ;
		Thou hast a choice of flight, I none. Thou choosest 	544 
‘To stand or fall, as stand or fall thy towers; 
	Priests may not choose; they stand or fall by shrines.
	Thus stand we both, or fall,
		Thou by the throne, and I beside the altar. 	548
But to thy child, ev’n in this funeral hour, 
	Give the sole lawful guardian failing thee;
	Let her free will elect
		From thy brave warriors him her heart most leans to;   	552
‘And pass with him along the secret way. 
	To lengthen yet the line of Scythian Kings,
	Meanwhile, since needs must be
		We trust to others this long-guarded secret.	 556
Choose one to whom I may impart the clue 
	Of the dark labyrinth; for a guide it needs;
	Be he in war well tried,
		And of high mark among the Nomad riders; 	560
Such as may say unto the antique tribes 
	With voice of one reared up among themselves,
	From walls of stone I bring
		Your King’s child to your tents; let Scythia guard her	564
‘Well do thy counsels please me,’ sid the King.
	‘I will convene to such penurious feast ,
	As stint permits, the chiefs
		Worthiest to be the sires of warlike monarchs:        	568
‘And, following ancient custom with the Scyths, 
	He unto whom my dughther, with free choice,
	The wine - cup brimming gives,
		Shall take my blessing and go hence her husband.   	572
‘But since, for guide and leader of the few 
	That for such service are most keen and apt,
	The man in war most tried,
		And with the Nomads most esteemed, is Seuthes,   	576
Him to thy skilled instructions and full trust 
	Will I send stright.   Meanwhile go seek my child,
	And, as to her all thought
		Of her own safety in mine hour of peril 	580
Will in itself be hateful, use the force 
	That dwells on sacred lips with blandest art;
	Say that her presence here
		Palsies mine arm and dulls my brin with terror;    	584
That mine own safety I consult in hers,
	And let her hopeful think, that, tho’ we part, 
	The same road opes for both;
		And if walls fail me, hers will be my refuge.	 588
Omartes spoke, and of his stalwart chiefs 
	‘Selecting all the bravest yet unwived,
	He bade them to his board
		The following night, on matters of grave import;     	592
To Seuthes then the secret he disclosed,
	And Seuthes sought the hiefarch, conned the elite.
	And third the darksome maze
		To either issue, sepulchre and palace; 	596
And thus instructed, treasu’re, town, and king 
	Thus in his hands for bargin with the foe
	The treason schemed of yore, 
		Foiled when the Mede rejected Scythian nuptials,   	600
Yet oft revolved - as some pale hope deferred, 
	Seen indistinct in rearward depths of time 
	Flashed as, when looked for least,
		Thro’ the rent cloud of battle flashes triumph.        	604
And. reasoning with himself, ‘the Mede’, he sid 
	‘Recks not who sits upon the Scythian throne,
	So that the ruler pay
		Grins of waste soil and drops of useless water:      	608
And if I render up an easy prey
	The senseless king refusing terms so mild,
	For such great service done
		And for my rank among the Scythian riders, 	612
‘The Mede would deem no man so fit as I
	To fill the throne; whose heir he scorned as wife,
	And yield him dust and drops,
		Holding the fealmS.and treasures of Omartes.’ 	616
So, when the next day’s sun began to slope,
	The tritor stood before Zariades, 
	Gaining the hostile camp
		From the mute grave.mound of his Scythian fathers. 	620
Plin as his simplest soldier’s was the tent 
	Wherein the lord of half the Orient sate,
	Alone in anxious thought,
		Intent on new device to quicken conquest. 	624
But for the single sapphire in his helm, 
	And near his hand the regal silver urn,
	Filled with the sparkling lymph,
		Which, whatsoe’er the distance, pure Choaspes      	628
Sends to the lips of Achemenian kings, 
	The Asian ruler might to Spartan eyes
	Have seemed the hardy type	
		Of Europe’s manhood crowned in Lacedemon.      	632
The tritor, sure of welcome, told his tale, 
	Proffered the treason and implied the terms.
	Then spoke Zariades;
		‘Know that all kings regard as foe in common       	636
‘The man who is a tritor to his king. 
	’Tis true that I thy treason must accept.
	I owe it to my hosts
		To scorn no means destroying their destroyer,        	640
‘But I will place no tritor on a throne. 
	Yet, since thy treason saves me many lives,
	I for their sake spare thine:
		And since thy deed degrades thee from the freeman, 	644
‘I add to life what slaves most covet - gold 
	They service done, seek lands where gold is king;
	And, tho’ thyself a slave,
		Buy freemen vile eno’ to call thee master.	648
But if thy promise fail, thy word ensnare, 
	Thy guidance blunder, by they side stalks death. 
	Death does not scare the man 
		Who, like thyself, has looked on it in battle;	652
‘But death in battle has a warrior’s gravel;
	A tritor dead - the vultures and the dogs.
	Then to close guard the King 
		Consigned the Scyth, who for the first time trembled;  	 656
And called in haste, and armed his Sacred Band,
	The Persian flower of all his Orient hosts;
	And soon in that .dark pass
		Marched war, led under rampired walls by treason.	660
Safe thro’, the fatal maze the Persians reached 
	Stirs winding upward into palace halls,
	With stealthy hand the guide,
		Pressed on the spring of the concealed portal,         	664
And slowly opening, peered within: the space
	Stood void; for so it had been planned, that none
	Might, when the hour arrived
		Obstruct the spot at which escape should vanish:    	668
But farther on, voices were heard confused, 
	And lights shone faintly thro’ the chinks of doors, 
	Where one less spacious hall 
		Led, also void, to that of fated banquet.	672
Curious, and yielding to his own bold heart, 
	As line on line came, steel-clad, from the wall,
	Flooding funereal floors, 
		The young King whispered, ‘Here await my signal,’ 	676
And stole along the intervening space, 
	At whose far end, curtins of Lydian woof,
	Between vast columns drawn,
		Left a slight crevice where their folds disparted;      	680
He looked within, unseen; all eyes were turned 
	Towards a pale front, just- risen o’er the guests,
	In which the Persian knew
		His brother King; it was not pale in battle. 	684
And thus Omartes spoke: - ‘Captins and sons 
	Of the same mother, Scythia, to this feast,
	Which in such strits of want
		Needs strong excuse, not idly are ye summoned.     	688
Wishing the line of kings from which I spring 
	Yet to extend, perchance, to happier times,
	And save mine only child
		From death, or, worse than death, the Median bondage,  	692
‘I would this night betroth her as a bride
	To him amongst you whom herself shall choose;
	And the benignant gods
		Have, thro’ the wisdom of their sacred augur,        	696
‘Shown me the means which may elude the foe, 
	And lead the two that in themselves unite
	The valour and the sway
		Of Scythia where her plins defy besiegers. 	700
If the gods bless the escape they thus permit,
	Braved first, as fitting, by a child of kings,
	Then the same means will free
		Flight for all those who give to siege its terror;       	704
Women and infants, wounded men and old, 
	If few by few, yet night by night, sent forth,
	Will leave no pang in death
		To those reserved to join the souls of heroes.        	708
As, in the hush of eve, a sudden wind
	Thrills thro’ a grove and bows the crest of pines,
	So crept a murmured hum
		Thro’ the grave banquet, and plumed heads bent downward: 	712
Till hushed each whisper, and uprised each eye, 
	As from a door behind the royal dis
	Into the conclave came
		The priest Teleutias leading the King’s dughter.     	716
‘Lift up thy veil, my child, Argiope,’ 
	Omartes sid.   ‘And look around the board,
	And from yon beakers fill
		The cup I kiss as in thy hand I place it.	720
‘And whosoever from that hand receives 
	The cup, shaH. be thy husband and my son.’
	The virgin rised her veil;
		Shone on the hall the starlight of her beauty. 	724
But to no face amid the breathless guests
	Turned downcast lids from which the tears dropped slow:
	Passive she took the cup,
		With passive step led by the whispering augur 	728
Where, blazing lustre back upon the lamps, 
	Stood golden beakers under purple pall. 
	‘Courage, ‘sid low the priest,
		‘So may the gods, for thy sake, save thy father !’   	732
She shivered as he spoke, but, lips firm - prest 
	Imprisoning all the anguish at her heart, 
	She filled the fatal cup, 
		Raised her sad eyes, and vaguely gazed around her.	736
Sudden those eyes took light and joy and soul, 
	Sudden from neck to temples flushed the rose, 
	And with quick, gliding steps, 
		And the strange looks of one who walks in slumber,	740
She passed along the floors, and stooped above
	A form, that, as she neared, with arms outstretched, 
	On bended knees sunk down 
		And took the wine - cup with a hand that trembled: 	744
A form of youth - and nobly beautiful 
	As Dorian models for Ionian gods. 
	‘Agin!’ it murmured low, 
		‘O dream, at last! at last! how I have missed theel’ 	748
And she replied, ‘The gods are merciful, 
	Keeping me true to thee when I despired.
	But now from every guest
		Burst the quick cry of anger and of terror;	752
For rose the kneeler, rose that sovereign front 
	Dire to the lives of men as Mars, the star; 
	Zariades the Mede!
		Rang from each lip: flashed from, each sheath the sabre:	756
Thrice stamped the Persian’s foot: to the first sound 
	Ten thousand bucklers echoed back a clang; 
	The next, and the huge walls 
		Shook with the war-shout of ten thousand voices;   	760
The third, and as between divided cloud 
	Flames fierce with deathful pest an angry sun, 
	The folds, flung rudely back, 
		Disclosed behind one glare of serried armour.         	764
On either side, the Persian or the Scyth, 
	The single lord of life and death to both, 
	Stayed, by a look, vin strife;
		And passing onward amid swords uplifted, 	768
A girl’s slight form beside him his sole guard, 
	He pused before the footstool of the King, 
	And in such tones as soothe 
		The wrath of injured fathers, sid submissive -	772
I have been guilty to the gods and thee 
	Of man’s most sinful sin, - ingratitude; 
	That which I pined for most 
		Seen as a dream, my waking life rejected; 	776
Now On my knees that blessing I implore.
	Give me thy dughter; but a son receive, 
	And blend them both in one 
		As the mild guardian of the Scythian River.’	780

A BALLAD
By
OLIVER GOLDSMITH
		 “	Turn, gentle hermit of the dale,
	And guide my lonely way, 
To where yon taper cheers the vale. 
	With hospitable ray.
		 “	For here, forlorn and lost tread, 
	With fainting steps and slow; 
Where .wilds immeasurably spread, 
	Seem lengthening as I go”.
		 “	Forbear, my son,” the hermit cries, 
	“To tempt the dangerous gloom;
For yonder faithless phantom flies 
	To lure thee to thy doom.
		 “	Here to the houselesss child of want,
	My door is open still; 
And tho’ my portion is but scant,
	I give it with good will.
		 “	Then turn to-night, and freely share 
	Whate’er my cell bestows;
My rushy couch, and frugal fare, 
	My blessing and repose.
		 “	No flocks that range the valley free,
	To slughter I condemn: 
Taught by that power that pities me,
	I learn to pity them.
		 “	But from the mountin’s grassy side
	A guiltless feast I. bring; 
A scrip with herbs and fruits supply’d,
	And water from the prisng.
		 “	Then, pilgrim, turn, thy cares forego;
	All earth-born cares are wrong: 
Man wants but little here below,
	Nor wants that little long.”
			Soft as the dew from heav’n descends,
	His gentle accents fell; 
The modest stranger lowly bends,
	And follows to the cell.
			Far in a wilderness obscure
	The lonely mansion lay, 
A refuge to the neighbouring poor
	And strangers led astray.
			No Stores beneath its humble thatch 
	Requir’d a master’s care;
The wicket opening with a latch, 
	Receiv’d the harmless pir.
			And now when busy crowds retire 
	To take their evening rest,
The hermit trimm’d his little fire, 
	And cheer’d his pensive guest;
			And spread his vegetable store, 
	And gayly prest, and smil’d,
And skill’d in legendary lore. 
	The lingering hours bcguil’d.
			Around in sympathetic mirth 
	Its tricks the kitten tries,
The cricket chirrups in the hearth; 
	The crackling faggot flies.
			But nothing could a charm impart 
	To sooth the stranger’s woe;
For grief was heavy at his heart, 
	And tears began to flow.
			His rising cares the hermit spy’d, 
	With answering care opprest:
		 “	And whence, unhappy youth,” he cry’d, 
	“The sorrows of thy breast ?
		 “	From better habitations spurn’d,
	Reluctant dost thou rove; 
Or grieve for friendship unreturn’d,
	Or unregarded love?
		 “	Alas! the joys that fortune brings
	Are triflingi and decay; 
And those who prize the paltry things,
	More trifling still than they.
		 “	And what is friendship but a name,
	A charm that lulls to sleep; 
A shade that follows wealth or fame,
	But leaves the wretch to weep ?
		 “	And love is still an emptier sound,
	The modern fair one’s jest, 
On earth unseen, or only found
	To warm the turtle’s nest.
		 “	For shame, fond youth, thy sorrows hush, 
	And spurn the sex,” he sid:
But, while he spoke, a rising blush 
	His love-lorn guest betray’d.
			Surpriz’d he sees new beauties rise 
	Swift mantling to the view,
Like colours o’er the morning skies, 
	As bright, as transient too.
			The bashful look, the rising breast,
	Alternate spread alarms, 
The lovely stranger stands confest
	A mid in all her charms.
			And “ah, forgive a stranger rude, 
	A wretch forlorn,” she cry ‘d,
		 “	Whose feet unhallowed thus intrude 
	Where heaven and you reside.
		 “	But let a mid thy pity share. 
	Whom love has rught to stray;
Who seeks for rest, but finds despir 
	Companion of her way.
		 “	My father liv’d beside the Tyne,
	A wealthy lord was he; 
And all his wealth was Vark’d as mine,
	He had but only me.
		 “	To win me from his tender arms.
	Unnumber’d suitors came; 
Who pris’d me for imputed charms,
	And felt or feign’d flame.
		 “	Each hour a mercenary crowd
	With richest proffers strove,
Amongst the rest young Edwin bow’d, 
	But never talk’d of love.

		ittokutiyil caṅka kāla tamiḻ maṉṉarkaḷ kuṟitta ilakkiyam maṟṟum āvaṇaṅkaḷ cārnta varalāṟṟu āyvukaḷ iṭampeṟṟuḷḷaṉa. ipporuḷil  taṉi nūlāka ippōtutāṉ tokukkappaṭukiṉṟatu. palvēṟu nūlkaḷil iṭampeṟṟavi iṅku orucēra uḷḷaṉa. 
tokuti - 2	:	paṇṭit tamiḻaka varalāṟu:  
koṅku nāṭu - pāṇṭiyar - pallavar -  ilaṅki varalāṟu
		paṇṭit tamiḻakattil koṅku pakuti taṉit taṉmi yōṭu viḷaṅkiya pakutiyākum. mayili cīṉi. vēṅkaṭa cāmi avarkaḷ koṅku pakutikaḷ kuṟittu ceyta āyvukaḷ iṅku tokukkappaṭukiṉṟaṉa. pallava maṉṉarkaḷ paṟṟit taṉittaṉiyāṉa nūlkaḷi mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ eḻutiṉār. ivaṟṟiliruntu maṉṉarkaḷ kuṟittu varalāṟukaḷ maṭṭum  ittokutiyil tokukkap paṭukiṉṟaṉa. pāṇṭiyarkaḷ kuṟitta takavalkaḷum ittokutiyil iṭam peṟṟuḷḷatu. ilaṅki tamiḻar varalāṟu kuṟittup pala iṭaṅkaḷil mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ eḻutiyuḷḷavi ittokutiyil iṇikkappaṭukiṉṟaṉa. 
tokuti - 3	:	paṇṭit tamiḻaka varalāṟu: 
kaḷappirar - tuḷu nāṭu
		iruṇṭa kālam eṉṟu kūṟappaṭṭa varalāṟṟil oḷi pāycciya āyvu kaḷappirar paṟṟiya āyvu ākum. iṉṟiya karnāṭakap pakutiyiluḷḷa tuḷu nāṭu paṟṟiyum ivar eḻutiyuḷḷār. ivviraṇṭu nūlkaḷum it tokutiyil iṭampeṟukiṉṟaṉa.
tokuti - 4	:	paṇṭit tamiḻakam:
vaṇikam - nakaraṅkaḷ maṟṟum  paṇpāṭu
		mayili cīṉi palvēṟu taruṇaṅkaḷil eḻutiya paṇṭit tamiḻarkaḷiṉ vaṇikam, paṇṭit tamiḻaka nakaraṅkaḷ maṟṟum palvēṟu paṇpāṭṭuc ceytikaḷ ittokutiyil tokukkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa.
tokuti - 5	:	paṇṭit tamiḻakam: 
āvaṇam - pirāmi eḻuttukaḷ - naṭukaṟkaḷ
		tamiḻarkaḷiṉ tolleḻuttiyal toṭarpāṉa āyvukaḷ tamiḻil mikakkuṟivē. kaḷāyvu mūlam mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ kaṇṭaṟinta pirāmi eḻuttukkaḷ maṟṟum naṭukaṟkaḷ toṭarpāṉa āyvukaḷ ittokutiyil cērkkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa.
tokuti - 6	:	paṇṭit tamiḻ nūlkaḷ:  
kālārāycci  - ilakkiya ārāycci
		paṇṭit tamiḻ nūlkaḷiṉ kālam paṟṟiya palvēṟu muraṇpaṭṭa āyvukaḷ tamiḻil nikaḻntuḷḷaṉa. mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ tamatu kaṇṇōṭṭattil tamiḻ ilakkiyaṅkaḷ kuṟittuc ceytu kāla āyvukaḷ it tokutiyil tokukkappaṭukiṉṟaṉa. tamiḻk kāppiyaṅ kaḷ maṟṟum pala ilakkiyaṅkaḷ kuṟittu mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ ceyta āyvukaḷum it tokutiyil iṭam peṟukiṉṟaṉa.
tokuti - 7	:	tamiḻakac camayaṅkaḷ: 
camaṇam
		‘camaṇamum tamiḻum’ eṉṟa poruḷil mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ eḻutiya nūl ittokutiyil iṭampeṟu kiṉṟatu. camaṇam kuṟittu ivar eḻutiya vēṟu pala kaṭṭurikaḷ it tokutiyil iṇikkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa. 
tokuti - 8	:	tamiḻakac camayaṅkaḷ:
puttam
		‘puttamum tamiḻum’ eṉṉum poruḷil ivar eḻutiya āyvukaḷ ittokutiyil iṭampeṟukiṉṟaṉa. puttam toṭarpāka palvēṟu iṭaṅkaḷil mayili cīṉi eḻutiya kaṭṭurikaḷ aṉittum ittokuti yil iṇikkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa.
tokuti - 9	:	tamiḻil camayam: 
kutamaputtariṉ vāḻkki
		puttariṉ varalāṟu putta jātakak katikaḷi aṭippaṭi yākak koṇṭu eḻutappaṭṭuḷḷatu. ivvakiyil mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ eḻutiya puttariṉ vāḻkki varalāṟu it tokutiyil iṭampeṟṟuḷḷatu. 
tokuti - 10	:	tamiḻil camayam:
puttak katikaḷ - icivāṇar katikaḷ
		1940 mutal puttak katikaḷi tamiḻil moḻi peyarttavar mayili cīṉi. puttak kalivāṇarkaḷiṉ vāḻkki varalāṟṟiyum ivar tokuttuḷḷār. iv viraṇṭu nūlkaḷum ittokutiyil iṭampeṟṟuḷḷaṉa. 
tokuti - 11	:	tamiḻil camayam:
putta jātakak katikaḷ
		putta jātakak katikaḷ tamiḻil palarāl moḻipeyarkkap paṭṭuḷḷaṉa. mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ ceytuḷḷa putta jātakak katikaḷiṉ moḻipeyarppu ittokutiyil iṭampeṟukiṟatu.
tokuti - 12	:	tamiḻakak kali varalāṟu:
ciṟpam - kōyil
		mayili cīṉi tamiḻaka ciṟpaṅkaḷ maṟṟum kōyilkaḷ kuṟittu virivāṉa āyvukaḷi mēṟkoṇṭuḷḷār. palvēṟu iṭaṅkaḷil iṭampeṟṟuḷḷa avvāyvu kaḷi ittokutiyil orucērat tokuttuḷḷōm.
tokuti - 13	:	tamiḻaka kali varalāṟu: 
		ici - ōviyam - aṇikalaṉkaḷ
		tamiḻarkaḷiṉ paṇmarapu kuṟittum tamiḻ nāṭṭu ōviyam kuṟittum virivāṉa āyvi ivar mēṟkoṇṭuḷḷār. ivvāyvukaḷ aṉittum ittokutiyil tokukkap paṭṭuḷḷaṉa. tamiḻarkaḷiṉ aṇikalaṉ kuṟittu mayili cīṉi. eḻutiyuḷḷa āyvukaḷum ittokutiyil iṭam peṟukiṉṟaṉa.
tokuti - 14	:	tamiḻaka āvaṇaṅkaḷ: 
cācaṉac ceyyuḷ - ceppēṭukaḷ - kalveṭṭukaḷ
		kalveṭṭukkaḷil  ceyyuḷkaḷ mikutiyāka eḻutap paṭṭuḷḷaṉa. ivi aṉittiyum tokuttu cācaṉac ceyyuḷ mañcari eṉṟa oru nūli mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ veḷiyiṭṭārkaḷ. tamiḻaka varalāṟu toṭarpāka ceppēṭukaḷil kāṇappaṭum virivāṉa takavalkaḷ paṟṟi ivar āyvu ceytuḷḷār. tamiḻil uḷḷa kalveṭṭukkaḷ toṭarpākavum mayili cīṉi avarkaḷuṭiya āyvukaḷ kuṟippiṭattakkavi. ivi aṉittum ittokutiyil iṇikkap paṭṭuḷḷaṉa. 
tokuti - 15	:	tamiḻaka āvaṇaṅkaḷ: 
maṟintupōṉa tamiḻ nūlkaḷ
		maṟintupōṉa tamiḻnūlkaḷ eṉṉum peyaril mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ nūl oṉṟi eḻutiyuḷḷār. cumār 250 maṟintu pōṉa nūlkaḷ paṟṟiya virivāṉa takavalkaḷi innūlil avar tokuttuḷḷār. annūl ittokutiyil iṇikkappaṭṭuḷḷatu.
tokuti - 16	:	tamiḻ ilakkiya varalāṟu: 
pattoṉpatām nūṟṟāṇṭu
		pattoṉpatām nūṟṟāṇṭu tamiḻ ilakkiyam eṉṉum peyaril mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ eḻutiya nūl ittokutiyil iṭampeṟukiṟatu. ipporuḷ kuṟittu tamiḻil uḷḷa ariya nūl ituveṉṟu kūṟumuṭiyum.
tokuti - 17	:	tamiḻ ilakkiya varalāṟu: 
kiṟittuvamum tamiḻum
		airōppiya iyēcu capikaḷ mūlamāka tamiḻakattiṟku varukippurinta pātiriyārkaḷ tamiḻukku ceyta toṇṭu aḷaviṭaṟpālatu. ippaṇikaḷ aṉitti yum ivar tokuttuḷḷār; maṟṟum tamiḻil accuk kalimūlam uruvāṉa palvēṟu putiya viḷivukaḷ kuṟittum mayili cīṉi eḻutiyuḷḷār. ivi aṉittum ittokutiyil tokukkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa.
tokuti - 18	:	tamiḻiyal āyvu: 
collāyvukaḷ - vāḻkki varalāṟu
		palvēṟu taruṇaṅkaḷil paṇpāṭṭuc ceytikaḷukkut taravāka coṟkaḷ amivatu kuṟittu mayili cīṉi avarkaḷ āyvu ceytuḷḷār. ivvāyvukaḷ it tokutiyil iṇikkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa. pala aṟiñarkaḷil vāḻkki varalāṟu kuṟittum ivar eḻutiyuḷḷār ac ceytikaḷum ittokutiyil iṇikkappaṭṭuḷḷaṉa.
tokuti - 19	:	patippu, moḻipeyarppu, uri: 
nēminātam - nantikalampakam -  piṟa
		nēminātam, nantikalampakam ākiyavaṟṟi ivar patippittuḷḷār. ‘mattavilācam’ eṉṉum camaskiruta nāṭaka nūli moḻipeyarttuḷḷār. innūlkaḷ it tokutiyil iṇikkappaṭukiṉṟaṉa. mayili nēmi nātar patikam eṉṟa oru nūliyum ivar patippit tuḷḷār. annūl ittokutiyil iṭampeṟukiṟatu. uṇavu muṟikaḷ kuṟittu ivar eḻutiya uḷḷa nūlum ittokutiyil iṇikkappaṭṭuḷḷatu.
tokuti - 20	:	patippu: 
maṉōṉmaṇiyam nāṭakam
		maṉōṉmaṇiyam cuntarappiḷḷi eḻutiya  maṉōṉ maṇiya nāṭakatti mayili cīṉi patippittuḷḷār. in nāṭakam kuṟitta virivāṉa āyvuriyiyum innūlil ceytuḷḷār. innūl ittokutiyil iṭampeṟukiṟatu. 

vī. aracu

kuṟippukaḷ
kuṟippukaḷ
kuṟippukaḷ

ciṅkātaṉam - ciṅkācaṉam. tikaḻtara - viḷaṅka. aṭiyiṉi - iraṇṭu pātaṅkaḷ. kaṭimalar - maṇamuḷḷa pū. āram - cantaṉam; pūmāli. kaḻikaṟi - karumpik kuṟikkum. koyyuḷi - koyyappaṭṭa (kattarikkappaṭṭa) piṭari mayir.
jaya vijayīparava-veṟṟi uṇṭāvatāka. tavarājēntirā tava rājarkaḷukku intiraṉ pōṉṟavaṉē. pavam-piṟam irintiṭa muṟintupōka. pīṭu-perumi. 

atta taliva kēṇmō - kēṭpīrāka, vātavūrar - tiruvātavūriṟ piṟantavar; māṇikkavācakar. “teṉpāṇṭi nāṭē civalōkam” māṇikkavācakar tiruvākku. “tilliyuḷ kūttaṉē teṉpāṇṭi nāṭṭāṉē”, “mīḷā aruḷpurivāṉ nāṭu eṉṟum teṉpāṇṭi nāṭē teḷi” eṉṟu tiruvācakattil kūṟappaṭṭiruppatu kāṇka.
48 mutal 51 variyil uḷḷa aṭikaḷil, nilulakattip pacuvākavum, paratakaṇṭatti (intiyā tēcatti appacuviṉ maṭiyākavum, teṉkōṭiyākiya pāṇṭināṭṭi maṭiyil uḷḷa kāmpākavum uruvakam ceykiṟār.
taraṇi - pūmi; nilam. paratam - pārata tēcam, caratam - uṇmi. curi-kāmpu, curattaliyuṭiyatu eṉṉum poruḷ uḷḷatu. kaṇṇutal-neṟṟik kaṇṇiyuṭiya civaperumāṉ. paṉivari-imayamali. tuli-tarācu.
kuṟumuṉi - akattiya muṉivar. malayam - malayamali, poṉvari - imaya mali. 55 mutal58 variyil uḷḷa aṭikaḷ purāṇak katiyik kuṟikkiṉṟaṉa. ataṉip purāṇakkati viḷakkattiṟ kāṇka.) 
	cannu - oru muṉivar peyar. ivar kātu vaḻiyākak kaṅki veḷippaṭṭatu. (ataṉip purāṇakkati viḷakkattiṟ kāṇka.) kāḻ akil - viram poruntiya akil maram. cāṭi - mōti, kuṅkumam - kuṅkuma maram. paṇi - vayal. vāram - ōram. ēyum-okkum.  aṭiyalar - pakivar.  kaṭcevi - pāmpu. cuṭiki - uccik koṇṭi. mañcu - mēkam. iñci - matil. uriñci-urāyntu,  utayaṉ - cūriyaṉ.  patāki - koṭi. 

uvāmati - muḻunilā.  mācu - kaḷaṅkam. 79, 80 - ām aṭikaḷ mīṉ uruvam eḻutappaṭṭa pāṇṭiyaṉuṭiya koṭi kāṟṟil acintāṭuvatu, muḻu nilāviṉuṭiya kaḷaṅkatti nakkuvatu  pōlirukkiṟatu eṉṉum karuttuḷḷatu. mīṉkaḷ pāci mutaliya aḻukkit tiṉpatu iyal pākaliṉ, nilāvil uḷḷa kaḷaṅkatti nakkuvatupōlirukkiṟatu eṉṟu kūṟiṉār.

eyiliṉam - kōṭṭiyiṉ matil, kottaḷam mutaliyavi. entirap paṭi, tantirak karuvi, poṟikaḷ - pakivar pōrukku vantāl avarkaḷi etirppataṟkākak kōṭṭic cuvarkaḷiṉmēl amikkappaṭum pōrkkaruvikaḷ. kiṟikaḷ - vañcaka vaḻikaḷ,   tuṉṉalar - pakivar.  veruvuvar - añcuvārkaḷ.    porutu - pōrceytu,  araṇkōṭṭi. puri - kuṟṟam; palaviṉam.  muṉamuṉam - mutaṉ mutalil.  āyaṟpāṟṟu -ārāyattakkatu.

viracamāy -  cuviyillāmal; viruppamillāmal.  tuṉi - veṟuppu.  kīṇṭu - kiḻittu,  tuṉṉiya - neruṅkiyirunta,  avicu - ōmattīyil coriyum ney.

“pittaṉ eppaṭic cuntararkku, otta tōḻaṉāy uṟṟaṉaṉ” eṉṉum aṭikku, ‘pittaṉākiya naṭēcaṉ cuntaramuṉivarukku eppaṭi otta naṇpaṉāka āṉāṉ’ eṉṟu oru poruḷum, ‘pittaṉākiya civa perumāṉ cuntaramūrtti nāyaṉārukku eppaṭi otta naṇpaṉāṉāṉ’ eṉṟu vēṟoru poruḷum tōṉṟuvatu kāṇka.

taṉṉayam eṇṇā - cuyanalam niṉikkāta. nāyaṉār - tiruvaḷ ḷuva nāyaṉār. tūkkiya kuṟaḷ - ārāyntu kūṟiya tirukkuṟaḷ. 268 aṭi. “puṟaṅkuṉṟi kaṇṭaṉiya rēṉum akaṅkuṉṟi mūkkiṟ kariyā ruṭittu” eṉṉum tirukkuṟaḷic cuṭṭukiṟatu iv vākkiyam. niṉ aruku - uṉ pakkattil. avar - ‘akaṅ kuṉṟi mūkkiṟ kariyār’. vāku - tōḷ. aiyuṟal - aiyappaṭuvatu.

iravi - ñāyiṟu, cūriyaṉ. ayirttu - aiyuṟṟu. kāyvaṉē - kōpippāṉō, erippāṉō. tam peyar viḷampi - tammuṭiya peyaric colli, atāvatu kākā eṉak kūvit tām kākaṅkaḷ, iruḷ alla eṉṟu colli. cūriyaṉ katirparappi iruḷi ōṭṭiyapōtu, karuniṟamuṭiya kākaṅkaḷ, taṅkaḷiyum iruḷ eṉṟu karutic cūriyaṉ ōṭṭuvāṉō eṉṟu añci kākā eṉṟu kūvit taṅkaḷ peyarit terivittaṉa eṉpatu karuttu.

kūṭamāy - maṟivāy. ivvayiṉ - ivviṭattil. kaṇṭuḻi - pārtta pōtu. muruku - maṇam, aḻaku. mantakācattoṭu - puṉmuṟuvalōṭu. aḻuṅ kali-varuntātē. viyarttam - vīṇ. yārkol - yārō? ukuttu - utirttu, cinti. viḻinta - virumpiya. vīvuṟum - keṭum. vampuri - vīṇ pēccu. añcutum - añcukiṟōm. vāraliyō - varamāṭṭāyā.

13 mutal 25 varikaḷ, ceṭikaḷiṉ iyaṟki vicittirattik kūṟukiṉṟaṉa. ceṭikaḷ tamatu pūkkaḷil uḷḷa tēṉip parukat tēṉī mutaliya pūccikaḷi varac ceytu avaṟṟiṉ mūlamākap pūntātukaḷik karuppiyiṟ cērppittuk kāykāyttu viti yuṇṭākki, ak kāykaḷiṉmēl uḷḷa cuṇiyiṉāl āṭu māṭu maṉitar paṟavikaḷ mutaliyavar mūlamāka vevvēṟiṭaṅkaḷil ceṉṟu vaḷarac ceykiṟa tāvaraṅkaḷiṉ iyaṟki vicittiram kūṟappaṭukiṟatu.

kaṇpaṉippa - kaṇṇīr tuḷikka. cīriya Jḷi - nuṇṇiya maṇal. veṭittu - poṭiyākki. īrttu - iḻuttu, munnīr maṭu - kaṭalākiya nīrnili. kālattaccaṉ - kālamākiya taccaṉ. cālat takum - peritum poruntum. ōrntu - uṇarntu. aṇṭayōṉi - cūriyaṉ.
34 mutal 59 aṭikaḷ, nīriṉ iyaṟki vicittirattik kūṟukiṉṟaṉa. āṟṟil ōṭukiṟa nīr kaṟkaḷi uṭittup poṭiyākkit taṉṉiṭam akappaṭṭa poruḷkaḷi ellām maṇal kalluṭaṉ aṭittuk koṇṭu pōyk kaṭalil cērkkiṟatu. mīṇṭum an nīrē cūriya veppattiṉāl āviyāka mēlē ceṉṟu mēkamāki maḻiyākap peytu aruviyākavum āṟākavum cuṉiyākavum ūṟṟākavum vāykkālākavum ōṭi ōyviṉṟi irāp pakalāka uḻikkiṉṟatu eṉṉum iyaṟkiyiṉ vicittirattik kūṟukiṉṟaṉa.

cāṭu - pāy. īṭṭiyatu - cēkarittatu. ēkuvaṉ - pōvēṉ. eḻili - mēkam. ivaṇ aṭintu - iṅkē vantu. nirantaram - eppoḻutum. nāṅkūḻp puḻu - maṇpuḻu, nākappūcci. pāṭu - uḻippu. ōvā - ōyāta. viḻumiya - ciṟanta. yākki - uṭampu. vētittu - māṟṟi.
65 mutal 80 aṭikaḷ, nāṅkūḻp puḻuviṉ iyaṟki vicittirattik kūṟukiṉṟaṉa. paṟavikaḷ pūccikaḷ taṉṉip piṭittut tiṉṉātapaṭi

maṇṇil maṟintu vāḻkiṟa nāṅkūḻp puḻu maṇṇik kiḷaṟi viṭukiṟatu. ataṉāl kāṟṟum veḷiccamum maṇṇil kalantu maṇ payir paccikaḷ naṉṟāka vaḷarvataṟku ēṟṟatākiṟatu. aṉṟiyum maṇṇuṭaṉ maṭkippōṉa ilikaḷiyum aḻukkukaḷiyum tiṉṟu jīraṇittu meḻukupōlākki atic ciṟuciṟu maṇ kaṭṭikaḷāka veḷippaṭutti nilatti urappaṭuttukiṟatu. ivvāṟu payirt toḻilāḷarukku ippuḻu peritum tuṇipurikiṟa iyaṟki vicittirattik kūṟukiṉṟaṉa.   
viḻuppukaḻ - ciṟanta pukaḻ. alakilā - elli illāta. ilakiya - viḷaṅkiya. ciṟṟāṭi -ciṟiya kaṇṇaṭi; cūriya kiraṇaṅkaḷi oṉṟākac cērttut tīyiyuṇṭākkukiṟa ciṟu kaṇṇāṭi. miyam - ciṟṟāṭi yiṉ naṭuviṭam. ciṟṟāṭiyiṉ naṭu miyam cūriya kiraṇaṅkaḷi oṉṟu cērppatupōla, uṭampiṉ miyamāka iruppatu maṉam.
87 mutal89 aṭikaḷ, ciṟṟāṭiyiṉ (Hand lens) miyam cūriya kiraṇaṅkaḷi oṉṟupaṭuttuvatu pōla, uṭampil citaṟiyuḷḷa kuṇaṅkaḷi maṉam oṉṟupaṭuttukiṟatu eṉpatik kūṟukiṉṟaṉa. tīyaṉ - iṅkup palatēvaṉik kuṟikkiṟatu.
kirupaṇaṉ - ulōpi. tīṉam - eḷiyavaṉ, ēḻi. tōṟṟam - kāṭci. paṭām - tuṇi. potiyil - potikimali. mukaṭu - ucci.  poṟ puṟu - aḻakuḷḷa. karu - uruvaṅkaḷi amikkum vārppaṭa accu (Mould). āṭakap perukku - urukkiya poṉ. arukkaṉ - cūriyaṉ.
95 mutal 98 varikaḷiṉ karuttu: potikimaliyiṉmēl cūriyaṉ puṟappaṭukiṟa kāṭci, nāḷ ākiya kammiyaṉ (cūriya veḷiccamākiya) urukkiya poṉṉi (potiki maliyākiya) accil (mūciyil) ūṟṟuvatupōlat tōṉṟukiṟatu eṉpatu.
patāki - koṭi. tokuti - kūṭṭam. aṭupaṭi - kollum cēṉi.

vañcittāḻici 1. añcalil - añcāta. arikāḷ - pakivarkaḷē. cañcitam - mikuntuḷḷatu, eñciyuḷḷatu. cañcitap peruvāḻvu - ceṉṟatupōka mītiyuḷḷa vāḻnāḷ.
vañcittāḻici 2. eñcalil - kuṟivillāta. mañcuḷa - aḻakuḷḷa. kaṭiñi - piccip pāttiram.
vañcittāḻici 3. pitir -mūtātiyar. tuñciya pitir - iṟantu pōṉa uyirkaḷ. ivarkaḷit teṉpulattār eṉpar. eḷ - iṅku pitirkaḷukku iṭum eḷ. iṟantavarkaḷiṉ cāntikkāka eḷḷum nīrum iṟippatu intukkaḷiṉ vaḻakkam. jē - jeyam, veṟṟi.

ārppu - āravāram. kaḻal - vīrakkaḻal; veṟṟipeṟṟa pōrvīrar itik kālil aṇivar. vayappaṭi - valimiyuḷḷa cēṉi. oḷi - polivu. vañcittoṭi - vañcip pūmāli; māṟṟaracarit tākkuvataṟkākac cellum vīrarkaḷ aṇivatu. itu paḻiya tamiḻaracariṉ pōr marapu. taṇṇumi poruvum - mattaḷam pōṉṟa. poruppu - mali. urumu - iṭi. ōti - ōci. ‘veṇkalap poruppil urumu vīḻnteṉṉa’ eṉpatu veṇkala malimēl iṭi uruṇṭatu pōla eṉṉum paḻamoḻi. veṇkalak kaṭiyil yāṉi pukuntatupōla eṉṟuṅ kūṟuvar.
tuṉṉiya - neruṅkiya. kuratta - kuḷampiṉi yuṭiya. koyyuḷi - kattarikkappaṭṭa piṭari mayir. tirikkaṭaṟ kūṭṭam - ali vīcukiṉṟa kaṭalpōṉṟa (kutirikaḷiṉ) kūṭṭam; kutirip paṭi. peymata mimmukil kūṭṭam - matanīrip poḻiyum karumēkam pōṉṟa (yāṉikaḷiṉ) kūṭṭam; yāṉip paṭi. vilōtaṉam - tukilkoṭi. acalattiraḷ - malikaḷiṉ kūṭṭam. iṅkut tērp paṭiyik kuṟikkiṉṟatu.  

vayappaṭi - valimiyuḷḷa cēṉi. paṭiṟaṉ - vañcakam. camintāṉ - toṭaṅkiṉāṉ. malayaṉ - maliya malikkut talivaṉ; cēraṉ. aṟivatu - colluvatu. maritta - cetta.
āmip palaki - āmiyiṉ vaṭivamāka aminta maṇi. av velli - appoḻutu. aṟivar - colluvār. iyampi - colli. valiyōr - periyōr. itu maliyāḷa nāṭṭu vaḻakkuc col.
166 mutal 168 aṭikaḷ: periya maṉitariṉ mukak kuṟi, maṉak kuṟikaḷ, avarkaḷ collāmalē avarkaḷ eṇṇiyatit terivikkum eṉṉum karuttuḷḷaṉa.  

cākki - cāṭci, kari. ētuvāka - kāraṇattiṉāl. tetteṭuttal - piḷḷip pēṟillātavar piṟaruṭiya piḷḷiyic cuvīkāram ceytal. kuṭakaṉ - cēraṉ. uṟutuyar - varukiṟa tuṉpatti. āṟṟār - poṟukkamāṭṭār. paṭiyōr - ulakattavar.
cāṟṟuvar - colluvārkaḷ. tēṟṟam - teḷivu. māṟṟam - col, pēccu. aṉumāṉittal - karutal aḷavi; kāriyattik kaṇṭu kāraṇattiyuṇartal. aḷavi - piramāṇam, taṉmi. aṅkāṭi - kaṭitteru. ampalam - palarum kūṭum iṭam.
209-ām vari. ‘araṇmaṉi irakaciyam aṅkāṭi paraciyam’ eṉṉum maliyāḷa nāṭṭup paḻamoḻiyik kuṟikkiṉṟatu.  

antaraṅkap poruḷ - irakaciyaṅkaḷ uḻiyuḷār - arukil uḷḷavar. aṭiyavum - muḻuvatum. aṟikuvam - colluvōm.

13 - 15 varikaḷ, araṇmaṉiyiliruntu āciramam variyil muṉivar curaṅkam tōṇṭuvatāl uṇṭākum aravattik kuṟikkiṉṟaṉa.
19 vari. kaṇṭatu kaṉavō? - nī kātal koḷḷak kāraṇamāyiruntatu kaṉavuk kāṭciyō?
kaṇṇuḷār - kaṇṇilē taṅki yirukkiṟār. cittira rēki - cittiralēki. ivaḷ, vāṇācuraṉ makaḷākiya uṣi eṉpavaḷiṉ tōḻi. maṉitar uruvattic cittiramāka

varivatil vallavaḷ. uṣi tāṉ kaṉaviṟ kaṇṭu kātal koṇṭa aniruttaṉi iṉṉāṉ eṉṟu aṟiyāmal varuntiya pōtu, cittiralēki avaṉ uruvatti ōviyamāka eḻutik kāṭṭiṉāḷ. av vuruvantāṉ tāṉ kātalittavaṉ eṉṟu kūṟa, cittiralēki taṉ māyā jālattiṉāl aniruttaṉi avaṉ uṟaṅkiya kaṭṭilōṭu koṇṭuvantu uṣiyiṉiṭam viṭṭāḷ eṉpatu purāṇak kati. nī cittiralēki alli - eṉpatu, cittiralēkipōla vāṇi ōviyam eḻuta vallavaḷ allāḷ eṉpatu
34 vari. paḻakiyum uḷiyē - vāṇi taṉ kātalaṉi nēril kaṇṭu paḻaki yirukkiṟāḷ eṉpatum, maṉōṉmaṇi taṉ kātalaṉi nēril kāṇāmal kaṉavil maṭṭum kaṇṭirukkiṟāḷ eṉpatum karuttu.
ceyyuḷ 1 .	aṭavi - kāṭu. pāti viṭuttu - vaḻi tavaṟi. aḷitta - pātukātta.  

ce. 2. vari - aḷavu, elli. vari kuṟiyātu niḻali aḷattal - taṉ niḻali aḷakka virumpiya oruvaṉ, an niḻaliṉ elliyik kuṟikkāmalē aḷakkat toṭaṅkiṉāl atu aḷavukku akappaṭāmal nīṇṭukoṇṭē pōkum eṉpatu. āriruḷ - niṟinta iruḷ. uṭala māṟum vaki - uṭalam āṟum vaki eṉṟum, uṭalam māṟum vaki eṉṟum pirittu iruporuḷ koḷka. vīṭu - illam, mōṭcam.
ce. 3. ēkāntam - taṉitta iṭam. peruṅ kakaṉam - periya veḷi. aṇiyār - cēra māṭṭārkaḷ. cinti aṟa - maṉam illi yākumpaṭi. pantam - ulakap paṟṟu, kaṭṭu.
ce. 4. paṇip pāy - alaṅkārap paṇi ceyyappaṭṭa pāy, tirumāl paḷḷikoḷḷum pāmpākiya pāy. pūaṇi - malar aṇi; piramaṉ irukkiṟa veṇṭāmariyākiya aṇi - uṉṉār - niṉikka māṭṭārkaḷ.
ce. 5. vētaṉi - tuṉpam. mey - uṭampu. calippu - kaḷippu. viṭiyum - nīṅkum, pōkum.   
ce. 6. ētō taṉ paḻa niṉivu eḻa - vaḻippōkkaṉukku muṉivarippaṟṟi ētō paḻiya niṉivu uṇṭāka. kaṇpaṉittu - kaṇṇīr kacintu.
ce. 7. iḻikka - ceyya. antaram - ākāyam. cuntaram -aḻaku. tārakikaḷ - viṇmīṉkaḷ. aṟintu - colli.
ce. 8. piṇinta - cērnta. aṉṟil - aṉṟiṟ paṟavi. ivi āṇum peṇṇum iṇipiriyāmal irukkum eṉṟum, pirintāl uṭaṉē uyirviṭum eṉṟum kūṟuvar. ivi kaṟpaṉip paṟavi. alarmalar - alarkiṉṟa malarkaḷ. kaṇṇīr aruvi - kaṇṇīr aruvi eṉṟum, kaḷ (tēṉākiya) nīr aruvi eṉṟum iruporuḷ koḷḷalām. akam uṭintu - maṉamuṭintu. tūva - coriya.
ce. 9. vinti - vitti. terikka - colla. viḷippavariṉ - kūppiṭu kiṟavarpōla. vāval - vuvāl paṟavi, vuvāl taṉatu tōl ciṟakukaḷi acittup paṟappatu kiyi acittu aḻippatu pōla irukkiṟatu. viḷakkoṭu - veḷiccattōṭu.  

anti - mālinēram. ēvi - pōkaviṭṭu. antiyi muṉ ēvi iraveṉṉum iṟivi vantiṟuttaṉaḷ - māli nēramākiya tōḻiyi muṉ pōkaviṭṭu iravākiya irāṇi avaḷ piṉṉē vantāḷ.
ce. 10. poriyaravu - puḷḷikaḷiyuṭiya pāmpu. cuṭiki - tali, ucci. polaṉmaṇi - polivuḷḷa māṇikka maṇi. pāmpukaḷiṉ taliyil māṇikka maṇi uṇṭeṉpatu kaviñarkaḷiṉ kaṟpaṉi. oḷi - veḷiccam. tiṇ - tiṇṇiya, palamuḷḷa. kaṟipaṭi - yāṉi, (uralpōṉṟa kāliyuṭiyatu). maruppu - tantam. uḻuvi - puli. aḻal viḻi - neruppup pōṉṟa kaṇkaḷ.
ce. 11. pērayarvu - atikat taḷarcci, maṉam iṟantu - maṉam kaṭantu.
ce. 12. oruṅkuāra - oṉṟāka neruṅkiyuḷḷa. muḷari- muṭkaḷ. uḻi - pakkam. ōṅkāra oli tarum kāṉanati - kāṭṭāṟukaḷiṉ nīroli ōṅkāra cattatti uṇṭākkiṉa.   

ce. 13. muṉivar namatu iruppiṭatti yaṭintapōtu aṅkē erintukoṇ ṭirunta neruppuc cuṭar, taliyit tāḻtti varavēṟṟatupōliruntatu.
ce. 14. nimalam - nirmalam, cuttamāṉa. meyppattar - uṇmiyāṉa paktar.
ce. 15. paṉippu - kuḷir, naṭukkam. palam - paḻam. mūlam - kiḻaṅku.
ce. 16. nāvil - nākkil. oru viralit tāṇṭutal - oru viraṟ kiṭi tūrattik kaṭattal. nāviṉāl kuṇṭali caktiyi eḻuppi oru viraṟkiṭi tūrattik kaṭantu amuta kaliyi uṇṇuvatu haṭayōka muṟi. itaṉi yōkikaḷ ceyvārkaḷ. cākaram - kaṭal. kantam - kiḻaṅku. aṉantam - pala.   

ce. 17. tirunta - cemmiyāka. uṟiti - taṅkuvāy. paṉippu - naṭukkam.
ce. 18. iyampiyum - colliyum. iciyāmal - collāmal. malivu - mayakkam. pakarntāṉ - coṉṉāṉ.
ce. 19. mukamati orukip patuma malar maṟippa - mukamākiya cantiraṉik kiyākiya tāmariyiṉāl maṟittāṉ. muṉivaruṭaṉ vanta vālipaṉ, uṇmiyil peṇmakaḷāki yiṉāl, tīyiṉ veḷiccattil taṉṉip peṇ eṉṟu terintu koḷḷātapaṭi mukattik kiyiṉāl maṟittāṉ eṉpatu karuttu. cuvar ōviyam - cuvaril eḻutappaṭṭa ōviyam. paṇṭik kālattil ōviyaṅ kaḷic cuvarkaḷilē eḻutiṉārkaḷ. karutiṭuvāṉ - eṇṇuvāṉ.
ce. 20. iravu vēḷiyil, tīccuṭar aṭaṅkuvatum poṅki erivatumāka irunta kāṭci, eliyip piṭitta pūṉi ataṉik koṉṟu tiṉpataṟku muṉṉar. avveliyi ōṭaviṭṭup piṭittu viḷiyāṭuvatupōla iruntatu eṉpatu ic ceyyuḷiṉ karuttu.
ce. 21. vatinta kuṟi - mukattil kāṇappaṭṭa kuṟi. eḻilārum iḷami - aḻaku poruntiya iḷamip paruvam. iṭiyūṟāti - tuṉpam mutaliyavi. ivaṇ - iṅkē.
ce. 22. ōtāy - colluvāy. kaḻaṉṟaṉaḷō - nīṅkiṉāḷō. mey viṭuttu - uṇmiyi veḷiyē viṭṭu. kaḻaṟāy - colluvāy.
ce. 23. viyakam - ulakam. kiyārum - kiyil varum. ilavu - ilava maram. ilavaṅkāy paḻukkātu. muṟṟiya piṟaku veṭikkum. paḻuttāl uṇṇalām eṉṟu kāttukkoṇṭirukkum kiḷi ēmāṟṟam aṭiyum. ākavē ‘ilavukātta kiḷi pōla’ eṉṉum paḻamoḻi vaḻaṅkukiṟatu. ic ceyyuḷiṉ mutal iraṇṭaṭikaḷiṉ karuttu, tī eriyumpōtu anta veḷiccattil poruḷkaḷiṉ niḻal kāṇappaṭṭu atu taṇiyumpōtu niḻalum maṟintuviṭuvatupōla ulaka cukaṅkaḷ nilittirā eṉpatu.

muyaṟkompu - ilporuḷ uvami. peruṅ kapaṭam iṭum kalaṉ - vañcakamākiya poruḷkaḷi iṭṭu vikkum pāttiram. piṟaṅkum - viḷaṅkum.
ce. 25. aṭikaḷ - muṉivar. ētilaṉ - vaṟiyavaṉ. veyiliṉmuṉ viḷakkoḷi eṉpatu paḻamoḻi. miṉṉeḷiyil kaṇpōla - miṉṉal veḷiccattil kaṇ kūcuvatupōla veḷippaṭṭa kaḷvaṉ - piṭipaṭṭa tiruṭaṉ.

- patiṉāṟu kalikaḷiyuṭiya cantiraṉ, nilā. kālkāṟṟu, pirintirukkum kātalarukku veṇṇilāvum, iḷaṅ kāṟṟum tuṉpam ceykiṟatu eṉpatu karuttu.

ce. 38. āyattār - tōḻip peṇkaḷ. āyavum - eṇṇip pārkkavum. akal vēli - paranta kaṭal. eṟiyum - alivīcum. akōrāttiram - pakalum iravum. kaṭal ōci pirinta kātalar kaḷukkut tuṉpam uṇṭākkum eṉpatu kavimarapu. kalimati  - patiṉāṟu kalikaḷiyuṭiya cantiraṉ, nilā. kālkāṟṟu, pirintirukkum kātalarukku veṇṇilāvum, iḷaṅ kāṟṟum tuṉpam ceykiṟatu eṉpatu karuttu.
